BrainAI EDUCATIONAL CORPUS
BATCH 11 — GRADE 12 ARTS / HUMANITIES (NCERT branch + Maharashtra HSC Arts branch)
Original teaching corpus; no textbook reproduced. Verified 2026: NCERT Class 12 continues existing books unchanged for 2026–27. Humanities: Political Science (Contemporary World Politics + Politics in India since Independence), Sociology (Indian Society + Social Change and Development in India), Psychology (attributes/intelligence, self & personality, stress, disorders, therapies, attitudes, group processes), History (Themes in Indian History I–III), Geography (Fundamentals of Human Geography + India: People and Economy), Economics (see Batch 9). Maharashtra HSC Arts (Std 12): History, Political Science, Geography, Sociology, Psychology, Economics, Logic (optional). Boards kept separate.
Per the Social-Science brief, this batch teaches theories and concepts and distinguishes FACT / INTERPRETATION / OPINION, presenting contested political questions evenhandedly. Fuller anchors: Psychology — Intelligence, Political Science — the Cold War & world order, Sociology — Indian society & social change; compact records for History, Geography, and the remaining psychology/politics chapters.

BRANCH A — NCERT / CBSE (Class 12 Humanities)
=================================================================

===== PSYCHOLOGY =====
Chapter arc: Variations in Psychological Attributes (Intelligence) · Self & Personality · Meeting Life Challenges (Stress) · Psychological Disorders · Therapeutic Approaches · Attitude & Social Cognition · Social Influence & Group Processes.

★ CHAPTER G12PSY-INT — Intelligence & Psychological Attributes — FULLER ANCHOR
1. METADATA
• Grade 12 · NCERT · Humanities · Psychology · Unit: Variations in psychological attributes · Prereq: Grade 11 methods & concepts · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: define intelligence & individual differences; compare major theories; understand IQ and its assessment; discuss culture, and concepts like aptitude, creativity, and emotional intelligence.
2. TEACHING MATERIAL
(a) Individual differences & intelligence. People differ in psychological attributes; intelligence is the ability to understand, learn from experience, reason, and adapt to the environment. Definitions vary by theory.
(b) Theories of intelligence.
 • Psychometric / Spearman's two-factor: a general factor ‘g’ underlies all intelligent behaviour, plus specific factors ‘s’.
 • Thurstone's Primary Mental Abilities: intelligence = several independent abilities (verbal, numerical, spatial, reasoning, etc.).
 • Gardner's Multiple Intelligences: eight relatively independent intelligences — linguistic, logical-mathematical, spatial, musical, bodily-kinaesthetic, interpersonal, intrapersonal, naturalistic.
 • Sternberg's Triarchic theory: componential (analytical), experiential (creative), contextual (practical) intelligence.
 • PASS model (Das): intelligence as Planning, Attention-arousal, and Simultaneous-Successive processing.
(c) IQ & assessment. IQ = (Mental Age / Chronological Age) × 100 (original ratio IQ). IQ scores form a roughly normal distribution (mean ≈ 100). Assessment uses verbal, non-verbal and performance tests, individual or group, and must be reliable, valid and culturally fair. Categories range from intellectual disability to giftedness.
(d) Culture & intelligence. Western views stress cognitive/analytical skills; Indian (e.g., buddhi) and other traditions include social, emotional and practical competence — intelligence is partly culturally defined.
(e) Related concepts. Aptitude (potential to acquire a skill), creativity (novel & useful ideas; not identical to IQ), emotional intelligence (perceiving, understanding and regulating emotions — EQ).

FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION. FACT: IQ tests yield scores distributed around 100. INTERPRETATION: Gardner argues intelligence is plural, not a single g. OPINION: "IQ tests are useless" — a value/empirical claim requiring evidence and nuance.

A. DEFINITIONS (10)
intelligence, ‘g’ factor, multiple intelligences, triarchic theory, IQ, mental age, aptitude, creativity, emotional intelligence, reliability.

B. QUESTIONS & ANSWERS
Easy: What does IQ stand for? (Intelligence Quotient.) Who proposed ‘g’? (Spearman.) Medium: Name Sternberg's three intelligences. (Analytical, creative, practical.) Difficult: How is ratio IQ computed? ((MA/CA)×100.) Application: A child, MA 10, CA 8 — IQ? ((10/8)×100 = 125.) Reasoning: Why must a good test be culturally fair? (To avoid bias against groups unfamiliar with the content.)

C. MCQs (12, abridged)
IQ = (MA/CA)×100; ‘g’ factor → Spearman; eight intelligences → Gardner; analytical/creative/practical → Sternberg; average IQ ≈ 100; potential to learn a skill = aptitude; novel & useful thinking = creativity; EQ concerns emotions; a consistent test is reliable; a test that measures what it claims is valid; intelligence distribution is roughly normal; PASS model by Das.

D. TRUE/FALSE (6)
Spearman proposed multiple intelligences. F (Gardner). IQ = (MA/CA)×100. T. Creativity always equals high IQ. F. EQ involves managing emotions. T. A valid test is one that gives consistent scores. F (that's reliability). Intelligence is defined identically across cultures. F.

E. FILL BLANKS (6)
IQ = (Mental Age/ Age)×100 (Chronological). Spearman's general factor is  (g). Gardner proposed  intelligences (multiple/eight). Sternberg's practical intelligence handles  life (everyday).  intelligence involves understanding emotions (Emotional). A dependable, consistent test is  (reliable).

F/G. SHORT/LONG
Short: Compute IQ for MA 12, CA 10. (120.) Long (model): "Compare the single-factor and multiple-intelligence views of intelligence." Model — Spearman's psychometric view holds a general factor ‘g’ underlies all intelligent performance, so one summary score is meaningful. Gardner challenges this, arguing for several relatively independent intelligences (linguistic, spatial, musical, interpersonal, etc.), so a single IQ misses a person's diverse strengths. This is an ongoing theoretical debate (interpretation), not a settled fact; each view has evidence and critics.

H. HOTS
Why might a highly creative person score only average on a standard IQ test? (IQ tests emphasise convergent, not divergent, thinking.) Can two people with the same IQ differ greatly in real-life success? (Yes — practical & emotional intelligence, motivation, opportunity matter.)

I. MISCONCEPTIONS
"IQ fully determines success" → many factors matter. "Intelligence is fixed and single" → theories dispute this; it's multidimensional and develops. "Creativity = intelligence" → related but distinct.

J. REVISION
Definitions & individual differences; theories (Spearman g, Thurstone PMA, Gardner MI, Sternberg triarchic, PASS); IQ & normal distribution; test qualities (reliable/valid/fair); culture; aptitude, creativity, EQ.

ADAPTIVE — "multiple intelligences"
L1: "There are many kinds of being smart, not just one." L2: "Gardner says people can be intelligent in different ways — with words, numbers, music, movement, understanding others, and more — so a single IQ number is too narrow." L3: "Gardner's theory posits several relatively autonomous intelligences with distinct neural and developmental profiles, challenging a unitary g." L4: "Intelligence is like a dashboard with many gauges — verbal, spatial, musical, social — not a single fuel meter." L5: "A gifted dancer, a math whiz, and an empathetic counsellor are each 'intelligent' in a different domain — that's multiple intelligences in real life."
``json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain the main theories of intelligence for a Class 12 student."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Several theories explain intelligence differently: (1) Spearman's TWO-FACTOR view — a general factor 'g' powers all intelligent behaviour, plus specific 's' factors. (2) Thurstone's PRIMARY MENTAL ABILITIES — several independent abilities like verbal and numerical. (3) Gardner's MULTIPLE INTELLIGENCES — eight kinds (linguistic, logical-mathematical, spatial, musical, bodily, interpersonal, intrapersonal, naturalistic). (4) Sternberg's TRIARCHIC theory — analytical, creative and practical intelligence. (5) PASS model — Planning, Attention, and Simultaneous-Successive processing. The debate over whether intelligence is one thing or many is an interpretive one, still discussed by psychologists."}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Calculate IQ if mental age is 10 and chronological age is 8."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Using ratio IQ = (Mental Age ÷ Chronological Age) × 100 = (10 ÷ 8) × 100 = 1.25 × 100 = 125. An IQ of 125 is above average (the mean is about 100). Note that modern tests use 'deviation IQ' comparing to age peers, but this ratio formula is the classic method taught here."}]}

===== POLITICAL SCIENCE =====

★ CHAPTER G12PS-COLDWAR — The Cold War & the Changing World Order — FULLER ANCHOR
METADATA. Grade 12 · NCERT · Political Science · Unit: Contemporary World Politics · Prereq: WWII basics · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: explain the Cold War & bipolarity; non-alignment; the end of bipolarity; US hegemony and emerging multipolarity — with FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION.
TEACHING.
• The Cold War (1945–1991). After WWII, two superpowers — the USA (capitalist bloc, NATO) and the USSR (communist bloc, Warsaw Pact) — competed globally without direct war (hence "cold"), through arms races, ideology, alliances and proxy conflicts (Korea, Vietnam, Cuba). Deterrence and the fear of mutual destruction (nuclear weapons) restrained direct war.
• Bipolarity. The world was largely organised around these two poles; smaller states often aligned with one.
• Non-Aligned Movement (NAM). Many newly independent nations (India, Egypt, Yugoslavia, Indonesia, Ghana — Nehru, Nasser, Tito) chose not to join either bloc, keeping independent foreign policy and promoting decolonisation and development. NAM was not neutrality/passivity but active engagement on their own terms.
• End of the Cold War. The USSR's economic weaknesses, reforms (glasnost, perestroika), and popular movements led to the collapse of the Soviet Union (1991) and the end of bipolarity.
• After bipolarity. The US emerged as the sole superpower (hegemony); globalisation deepened; new centres (EU, China, India, Russia) point toward multipolarity. Debates continue on US dominance, its limits, and a shifting order.
FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION. FACT: The USSR dissolved in 1991. INTERPRETATION: Historians debate the relative weight of economic vs ideological causes. OPINION: "A unipolar world is best for peace" — a contested value judgement.
DEFINITIONS (8). Cold War, bipolarity, superpower, deterrence, Non-Aligned Movement, hegemony, multipolarity, proxy war.
Q&A. Easy: Which two superpowers led the Cold War? (USA & USSR.) When did the USSR collapse? (1991.) Medium: What was NAM's stance? (Non-alignment with either bloc, independent policy.) Application: Why were conflicts like Vietnam called "proxy wars"? (Superpowers backed opposing sides without fighting each other directly.) Reasoning: Why did nuclear weapons restrain direct war? (Mutual assured destruction — deterrence.)
MCQs (8, abridged). Cold War poles: USA & USSR; military bloc of the West: NATO; India's stance: non-aligned; USSR collapse year: 1991; single dominant power: hegemony/unipolarity; fear-based restraint: deterrence; NAM leaders included Nehru, Nasser and Tito; conflicts fought via allies: proxy wars.
HOTS. Was non-alignment a sign of weakness or strategy? (A reasoned strategy for autonomy — present both views.) Is today's world unipolar or multipolar? (Contested — evidence for both; an interpretive debate.)
MISCONCEPTIONS. "Cold War had no fighting" → there were proxy wars, just not direct superpower war. "Non-alignment meant staying neutral/inactive" → it was active independent engagement.
REVISION. Cold War & bipolarity; NATO/Warsaw Pact; deterrence; NAM; 1991 collapse; US hegemony → emerging multipolarity; FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION.
ADAPTIVE — "non-alignment". L1: "Non-alignment meant not joining either superpower's side." L2: "Newly free nations like India refused to permanently join the US or Soviet bloc, keeping the freedom to decide each issue on its merits." L3: "Non-alignment was a foreign-policy doctrine asserting strategic autonomy and active agency for post-colonial states within a bipolar order." L4: "It's like refusing to permanently join either of two rival gangs at school — staying friendly-but-independent so you keep your own voice." L5: "India's ability to trade and talk with both blocs during the Cold War, without being anyone's satellite, is non-alignment in practice."

{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain the Cold War and non-alignment for a Class 12 exam."},{"role":"assistant","content":"The COLD WAR (1945–1991) was an intense global rivalry between two superpowers — the capitalist USA (with NATO) and the communist USSR (with the Warsaw Pact). They never fought each other directly (nuclear deterrence made that catastrophic), but competed through arms races, ideology, alliances and PROXY WARS (Korea, Vietnam). The world was BIPOLAR — organised around two poles. NON-ALIGNMENT was the response of many newly independent nations (India, Egypt, Yugoslavia) who refused to permanently join either bloc, preserving independent foreign policy. It wasn't passive neutrality but active engagement on their own terms. The USSR's collapse in 1991 ended bipolarity, leaving the US as the dominant power."}]}

===== SOCIOLOGY =====

★ CHAPTER G12SO-INDIA — Indian Society & Social Change — FULLER ANCHOR
METADATA. Grade 12 · NCERT · Sociology · Unit: Indian Society / Social Change & Development · Prereq: Grade 11 foundations · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: understand India's demographic structure; caste, tribe, class, family; social change processes; the impact of colonialism, industrialisation, urbanisation, globalisation; movements.
TEACHING.
• Demographic structure. Population size, growth, sex ratio, age structure; the demographic dividend (a large working-age population) and its challenges. Concepts: birth/death rate, fertility, migration.
• Social institutions in India. Caste (a system of hereditary, ranked groups; changing but persistent), tribe (distinct communities, often outside caste), family & kinship (joint vs nuclear; regional diversity), class (economic strata), gender (patriarchy and change).
• Structural change & colonialism. British rule reshaped the economy (deindustrialisation, land systems), introduced modern institutions, and created new classes.
• Processes of change. Sanskritisation (lower groups adopting higher-caste practices to raise status), Westernisation (adopting Western lifestyles/values), modernisation, secularisation, urbanisation and industrialisation — each with mixed effects.
• Development & its costs. Planned development, the Green Revolution (higher output, but regional/ecological concerns), industrialisation, displacement, and environmental impact.
• Social movements. Peasant, workers', women's, environmental, and caste/tribal movements as agents of change.
FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION. FACT: India's 2011 Census recorded specific population figures. INTERPRETATION: Sociologists debate whether caste is weakening or merely changing form. OPINION: "Urbanisation is destroying Indian culture" — a value judgement.
DEFINITIONS (10). demography, sex ratio, demographic dividend, caste, tribe, class, Sanskritisation, Westernisation, urbanisation, social movement.
Q&A. Easy: What is the sex ratio? (Females per 1000 males.) Define a social movement. (Organised collective effort for/against change.) Medium: What is Sanskritisation? Difficult: Distinguish Westernisation and modernisation. Application: How can a "demographic dividend" become a burden? (If jobs/education don't match the youth bulge.) Reasoning: Why say caste is "changing, not vanishing"? (Its ritual form weakens, but it persists in politics, marriage, inequality.)
MCQs (10, abridged). Females per 1000 males: sex ratio; large working-age population: demographic dividend; hereditary ranked groups: caste; adopting higher-caste practices: Sanskritisation; adopting Western ways: Westernisation; shift to city living: urbanisation; study of population: demography; Green Revolution raised agricultural output; organised push for change: social movement; joint vs nuclear concerns the family.
T/F (5). Sanskritisation is adopting Western dress. F. Sex ratio = females per 1000 males. T. A demographic dividend is automatically beneficial. F (needs jobs/education). Caste has completely disappeared. F. Social movements can drive change. T.
FILL BLANKS (5). Females per 1000 males is the  (sex ratio). Lower groups emulating higher castes =  (Sanskritisation). A youthful working-age bulge is the demographic  (dividend). Movement to cities is  (urbanisation). Organised collective action for change is a  (social movement).
HOTS. How can development simultaneously reduce poverty and cause displacement? (Dams/industry raise output but uproot communities — a trade-off.) Why might the same reform (Green Revolution) be judged success and failure? (Higher yields vs inequality/ecological costs — interpretive.)
MISCONCEPTIONS. "Caste = only religion" → it's a social-structural system affecting economy/politics. "Modernisation = Westernisation" → related but distinct. "Demographic dividend guarantees growth" → only if productively employed.
REVISION. Demography & dividend; caste/tribe/class/family/gender; colonial structural change; Sanskritisation/Westernisation/modernisation/urbanisation; development costs; social movements.
ADAPTIVE — "Sanskritisation". L1: "Lower-status groups copying higher-caste customs to raise their standing." L2: "Coined by M.N. Srinivas: a caste or group adopts the rituals, diet and lifestyle of a higher caste to claim higher social status over time." L3: "Sanskritisation is a process of positional mobility within the caste hierarchy via cultural emulation, without altering the structure itself." L4: "It's like adopting the manners of a more prestigious group to be accepted among them — climbing the ladder by imitation." L5: "A community shifting to vegetarianism or new rituals to be seen as higher-status illustrates Sanskritisation in Indian society."
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain Sanskritisation and Westernisation with the difference."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Both are processes of social change M.N. Srinivas described. SANSKRITISATION is when a lower or middle caste/group adopts the customs, rituals, diet and lifestyle of a higher caste to raise its social status WITHIN the caste system — positional mobility without changing the structure. WESTERNISATION is the adoption of Western lifestyles, values, technology and institutions (dress, education, ideas) due to contact with the West, especially under colonial rule. Key difference: Sanskritisation looks 'upward' inside Indian caste hierarchy; Westernisation looks 'outward' toward Western culture."}]}
``

BATCH 11 COVERAGE SUMMARY
• NCERT Psychology: Intelligence — fuller anchor (theories: Spearman/Thurstone/Gardner/Sternberg/PASS; IQ & distribution; test qualities; aptitude/creativity/EQ; culture) with FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION; compact for personality, stress/disorders/therapies (informational), attitude & group processes.
• NCERT Political Science: Cold War & world order — fuller anchor (bipolarity, NAM, deterrence, 1991, hegemony→multipolarity), evenhanded on contested points; compact for contemporary world politics and Indian politics since independence.
• NCERT Sociology: Indian society & change — fuller anchor (demography, caste/class/family/gender, Sanskritisation/Westernisation, development costs, movements); compact for social change & development.
• NCERT History/Geography: compact records stressing source interpretation & human-geography concepts; Economics cross-referenced to Batch 9.
• Maharashtra HSC Arts (Std 12): parallel subjects with Logic (symbolic logic/truth-tables) preserved as distinct and Maharashtra/India context foregrounded.
• Boards kept separate; FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION and balanced treatment maintained.

END OF BATCH 11. The Arts/Humanities stream (Grades 11–12) is now complete across both boards. Final: BATCH 12 — Maharashtra Bifocal / Vocational (Information Technology, Computer Science, Electronics, Electrical/Mechanical/Automobile Technology, Engineering Drawing, Office/Secretarial, Entrepreneurship — with theory, components, tools, procedures, practicals, troubleshooting, safety, viva & practical-exam questions).

BATCH 10 — GRADE 11 ARTS / HUMANITIES (NCERT branch + Maharashtra HSC Arts branch)
Original teaching corpus; no textbook reproduced. Verified 2026: NCERT Class 11 continues existing books unchanged for 2026–27. Humanities subjects: History (Themes in World History), Political Science (Political Theory + Indian Constitution at Work), Geography (Fundamentals of Physical Geography + India: Physical Environment), Sociology (Introducing Sociology + Understanding Society), Psychology (Introduction to Psychology), Economics (see Batch 8), and Philosophy/Logic where offered. Maharashtra HSC Arts (Std 11): History, Political Science, Geography, Sociology, Psychology, Economics, Logic/Philosophy (optional), languages. Boards kept separate.
Per the Social-Science brief, this batch teaches theories and concepts (not just lists them) and consistently distinguishes FACT / INTERPRETATION / OPINION. Depth policy: fuller conceptual anchors in Political Science (core political concepts), Sociology (foundational concepts), and Psychology (nature, methods, a core topic); compact-but-teaching records for History, Geography and Philosophy/Logic. Any topic expands on request.

BRANCH A — NCERT / CBSE (Class 11 Humanities)
=================================================================

===== POLITICAL SCIENCE =====
Books: Political Theory (concepts) and Indian Constitution at Work (institutions).

★ CHAPTER G11PS-CORE — Political Theory: Freedom, Equality, Rights, Justice — FULLER ANCHOR
1. METADATA
• Grade 11 · NCERT · Humanities · Political Science · Unit: Political Theory · Prereq: basic civics (Grade 9–10) · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: define politics and political theory; understand freedom (negative/positive), equality (kinds), rights (why & which), and justice (distributive) as reasoned concepts, not slogans; connect them to the Indian Constitution.
2. TEACHING MATERIAL
(a) What is politics & political theory? Politics is the activity through which people make, preserve and amend the general rules under which they live — involving negotiation, power, and the public good. Political theory examines the ideas and principles (freedom, equality, justice, rights, democracy) that underlie political life, clarifying their meaning and defending or criticising them with arguments.
(b) Freedom (liberty). Freedom is the absence of constraints and the presence of conditions for self-development.
 • Negative liberty — freedom from external interference (a protected private sphere).
 • Positive liberty — freedom to develop one's potential, needing enabling conditions (education, health).
 • Harm principle (J.S. Mill): a person's liberty may be restricted only to prevent harm to others; "self-regarding" acts should be free.
(c) Equality. The claim that all humans are of equal worth and deserve equal consideration.
 • Natural vs social inequalities: some differences (talent) vs those created by society (caste, class) — the latter are the concern of justice.
 • Kinds: political (equal vote/rights), economic (opportunity, reducing wealth gaps), social (dignity, non-discrimination).
 • Equality ≠ sameness: it means equal treatment of equals and, sometimes, differential treatment (affirmative action) to achieve real equality of opportunity.
(d) Rights. Justified claims of individuals, recognised by society/state, essential for a life of dignity.
 • Why rights? They protect vital human interests (life, liberty, expression) and set limits on state power.
 • Kinds: political, economic, cultural; legal rights vs human/moral rights. Rights carry duties (my right to free speech implies others' duty not to silence me).
(e) Justice. Giving each their due. Distributive justice concerns the fair distribution of benefits and burdens. Rawls' "veil of ignorance": just principles are those we would choose if we didn't know our own place in society — leading to protecting the least advantaged.
(f) Link to the Indian Constitution. These concepts are embedded as Fundamental Rights (equality, freedom, against exploitation, religion, cultural, remedies), Directive Principles (social/economic justice goals), and the Preamble (justice, liberty, equality, fraternity).

FACT vs INTERPRETATION vs OPINION.
 • FACT: The Indian Constitution guarantees the right to equality (Articles 14–18).
 • INTERPRETATION: Scholars argue reservations are a means to substantive equality.
 • OPINION: "Complete economic equality is the ideal society" — a value judgement.

A. DEFINITIONS (10)
politics, political theory, negative liberty, positive liberty, harm principle, equality, distributive justice, rights, duty, veil of ignorance.

B. QUESTIONS & ANSWERS
Easy: What is negative liberty? (Freedom from interference.) Which principle limits liberty to prevent harm? (Harm principle.) Medium: Distinguish natural and social inequalities. Why do rights imply duties? (Others must respect them.) Difficult: Explain Rawls' veil of ignorance. Application: Is affirmative action consistent with equality? (Yes — it aims at real equality of opportunity.) Reasoning: Why is "equality" not the same as "sameness"? (Equals treated equally; unequal starting points may need differential support.)

C. MCQs (10, abridged)
Freedom from interference = negative liberty; freedom to develop = positive liberty; harm principle is from J.S. Mill; giving each their due = justice; Rawls used the veil of ignorance; rights protect vital interests; every right implies a duty; reducing wealth gaps concerns economic equality; equal vote = political equality; the Preamble lists justice, liberty, equality and fraternity.

D. TRUE/FALSE (6)
Negative liberty means freedom to self-develop. F (that's positive). Rights come with duties. T. Equality means treating everyone identically in all cases. F. Justice means giving each their due. T. The harm principle allows restricting self-regarding acts. F. Political theory only lists rulers. F.

E. FILL BLANKS (6)
Freedom from interference =  liberty (negative). The  principle restricts liberty to prevent harm to others (harm). Rawls' thought experiment is the  of ignorance (veil). Every right implies a  (duty).  justice concerns fair distribution (Distributive). The Preamble seeks justice, liberty, equality and  (fraternity).

F/G. SHORT/LONG
Short: Differentiate negative and positive liberty with an example. Long (model): "Discuss whether affirmative action is compatible with equality." Model — Formal equality (identical treatment) can perpetuate disadvantage when starting points differ. Substantive equality aims at genuine equality of opportunity; affirmative action (e.g., reservations) temporarily gives differential support to historically disadvantaged groups to level the field. Critics interpret it as reverse discrimination; defenders see it as required by real equality. (Present as reasoned positions; the conclusion is contested — an interpretive debate, not a settled fact.)

H. HOTS
Can a society be free but unequal, or equal but unfree? (Yes — liberty and equality can tension; theories balance them differently.) Why might unlimited freedom threaten equality? (The powerful could dominate the weak absent limits.)

I. MISCONCEPTIONS
"Freedom means doing anything" → it's constrained by others' rights/harm. "Equality means everyone is identical" → equal worth/consideration, not sameness. "Rights are unlimited" → they carry duties and reasonable restrictions.

J. REVISION
Politics & political theory; negative/positive liberty & harm principle; kinds of equality (political/economic/social); rights & duties; distributive justice & Rawls; constitutional embedding; FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION.

ADAPTIVE — "positive vs negative liberty"
L1: "Negative liberty is being left alone; positive liberty is being able to actually do things." L2: "Negative liberty removes barriers (no one stops you from studying); positive liberty provides means (a school you can afford) so the freedom is real." L3: "Negative liberty is the absence of external constraint on the agent; positive liberty is the presence of enabling conditions for self-realisation — a distinction from Berlin's 'Two Concepts of Liberty.'" L4: "Negative liberty is an open door; positive liberty is also having legs to walk through it." L5: "Free speech laws (negative) matter little without literacy and internet access (positive) to actually be heard."

POSITIVE liberty is freedom TO develop yourself — having the actual conditions (education, health, opportunity) to act meaningfully; think 'able to become what I can be.' A person legally free to start a business (negative) may still lack the capital or skills to do so (positive). Good societies protect both."}]}

===== SOCIOLOGY =====

★ CHAPTER G11SO-FOUND — Foundational Sociological Concepts — FULLER ANCHOR
METADATA. Grade 11 · NCERT · Sociology · Prereq: none · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: define sociology & its perspective; understand society, social structure, socialisation, social institutions, status/role, culture; distinguish sociology from common sense.
TEACHING.
• What is sociology? The systematic study of human society and social behaviour — how groups, institutions and structures shape individuals and vice versa. The sociological perspective ("imagination", C. Wright Mills) links personal troubles to public issues (e.g., one person's unemployment vs a society-wide recession). It differs from common sense by using evidence, theory and method.
• Society & social structure. Society is an organised web of social relationships; social structure is the relatively stable patterning of these relationships (institutions, groups, roles) that persists beyond individuals.
• Socialisation. The lifelong process by which individuals learn the norms, values and roles of their society — via agents: family (primary), school, peer group, media, workplace. It makes culture continue across generations.
• Status & role. A status is a social position (student, mother); a role is the expected behaviour attached to it. Ascribed status (given by birth, e.g., caste, sex) vs achieved status (earned, e.g., doctor).
• Social institutions. Enduring patterns organising key needs — family & kinship, economy, polity, religion, education. They provide stability but can also reproduce inequality.
• Culture, norms, values. Culture = shared ways of living (material & non-material). Norms = rules of behaviour; values = shared ideas of the desirable; mores (strong moral norms) vs folkways (everyday customs).
• Founders & perspectives (intro): Comte (coined 'sociology'), Durkheim (social facts, solidarity), Marx (class conflict), Weber (interpretive understanding, verstehen).
FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION. FACT: Durkheim studied suicide rates sociologically. INTERPRETATION: He argued suicide reflects social integration. OPINION: "Modern society is worse than traditional society" — a value claim.
DEFINITIONS (10). sociology, sociological imagination, society, social structure, socialisation, status, role, ascribed status, social institution, norm.
Q&A. Easy: Who coined 'sociology'? (Comte.) Primary agent of socialisation? (Family.) Medium: Difference between ascribed and achieved status? Application: Is unemployment a personal trouble or public issue? (Both — sociological imagination links them.) Reasoning: Why is socialisation essential for society? (Transmits culture; without it, norms wouldn't persist.)
MCQs (10, abridged). Coined 'sociology': Comte; caste is an  status: ascribed; expected behaviour of a position: role; lifelong learning of norms: socialisation; family is the  agent: primary; strong moral norms: mores; 'social facts' associated with: Durkheim; class conflict with: Marx; verstehen with: Weber; stable patterning of relations: social structure.
T/F (5). Sociology is just common sense. F. Status is a social position. T. Achieved status is fixed at birth. F. Family is a primary agent of socialisation. T. Norms are shared ideas of the desirable. F (that's values).
FILL BLANKS (5).  coined the term sociology (Comte). Behaviour expected of a status is a  (role).  links personal troubles to public issues (sociological imagination). Learning society's norms is  (socialisation). Caste is an  status (ascribed).
HOTS. Why can the same individual hold several statuses that conflict (role conflict)? (Multiple roles make competing demands.) How might an institution both stabilise and perpetuate inequality? (e.g., education transmits skills but can reproduce class advantage.)
MISCONCEPTIONS. "Sociology = social work" → it studies society scientifically. "Individuals act purely freely" → structures shape choices. "Status means prestige only" → it's any social position.
REVISION. Sociology & its imagination; society/structure; socialisation & agents; status/role, ascribed/achieved; institutions; culture/norms/values; founders.
ADAPTIVE — "sociological imagination". L1: "It's seeing how society shapes your personal life." L2: "It links private troubles (I'm jobless) to public issues (the economy shed jobs) — your biography meets history and society." L3: "Mills' concept: the capacity to situate individual experience within broader social-structural and historical contexts." L4: "It's like zooming out from your single dot to see the whole pattern of dots you're part of." L5: "Understanding that your friend's stress before board exams is partly a society-wide pressure, not just personal, is the sociological imagination at work."
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"What is sociology and how is it different from common sense?"},{"role":"assistant","content":"Sociology is the systematic, evidence-based study of human society — how groups, institutions and social structures shape people, and how people shape them. Unlike COMMON SENSE (which relies on personal experience and taken-for-granted assumptions), sociology uses theories, careful methods and data, and questions what we assume is 'natural'. For example, common sense might say 'poverty is due to laziness,' but sociology examines structural causes — unemployment, education access, discrimination — using evidence. The key tool is the 'sociological imagination': connecting personal troubles to public issues."}]}

===== PSYCHOLOGY =====

★ CHAPTER G11PSY-INTRO — Nature, Methods & Core Concepts of Psychology — FULLER ANCHOR
METADATA. Grade 11 · NCERT · Psychology · Prereq: none · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: define psychology; its goals & subfields; scientific methods; understand a core process (learning) and key concepts (behaviour, cognition, the biological basis).
TEACHING.
• What is psychology? The scientific study of behaviour and mental processes (thinking, feeling, perceiving). Goals: describe, explain, predict and (help) control/change behaviour. It is a science because it uses systematic, empirical methods.
• Fields: cognitive, developmental, social, clinical, educational, organisational, health, and more.
• Methods: observation, experiment (manipulating an independent variable to see its effect on a dependent variable, with control), correlation (relationship, not causation), case study, survey, psychological testing. Ethics: informed consent, confidentiality, no harm.
• Perspectives (schools): psychodynamic (Freud — unconscious), behaviourist (Watson/Skinner — observable behaviour/learning), humanistic (Maslow/Rogers — growth), cognitive (mental processing), biological (brain/nervous system).
• Biological basis: the neuron, nervous system (central & peripheral), the brain's major parts; behaviour has a physical substrate.
• A core process — learning: a relatively permanent change in behaviour due to experience.
 • Classical conditioning (Pavlov): a neutral stimulus paired with a natural one comes to trigger a response (bell → salivation).
 • Operant conditioning (Skinner): behaviour is shaped by consequences — reinforcement (increases behaviour) and punishment (decreases it).
 • Observational learning (Bandura): learning by watching models.
FACT/INTERPRETATION/OPINION. FACT: Pavlov's dogs salivated to a bell after pairing. INTERPRETATION: This shows associative learning. OPINION: "Rewards are always better than punishment" — a value/empirical claim needing evidence.
DEFINITIONS (10). psychology, behaviour, cognition, independent variable, dependent variable, correlation, classical conditioning, operant conditioning, reinforcement, observational learning.
Q&A. Easy: Psychology studies behaviour and ? (Mental processes.) Who studied classical conditioning? (Pavlov.) Medium: Difference between reinforcement and punishment? Difficult: Why does correlation not imply causation? (A third variable or reverse direction may explain it.) Application: Giving a child a star for homework uses? (Positive reinforcement.) Reasoning: Why is control important in an experiment? (To isolate the effect of the independent variable.)
MCQs (10, abridged). Psychology is the science of behaviour and mental processes; Pavlov → classical conditioning; Skinner → operant conditioning; a reward that increases behaviour = reinforcement; watching a model = observational learning; variable manipulated = independent; relationship without causation = correlation; Freud → psychodynamic; Maslow → humanistic; the basic unit of the nervous system = neuron.
T/F (5). Psychology is not a science. F. Reinforcement decreases behaviour. F (increases). Correlation proves causation. F. Classical conditioning was studied by Pavlov. T. The neuron is a nervous-system cell. T.
FILL BLANKS (5). Psychology studies behaviour and  processes (mental).  conditioning pairs stimuli (Classical).  increases a behaviour (Reinforcement). The variable manipulated is the  variable (independent). Bandura studied  learning (observational).
HOTS. A study finds ice-cream sales and drowning both rise together — does ice-cream cause drowning? (No — a third variable, summer heat.) Why might punishment have side effects that reinforcement doesn't? (Fear/aggression; it doesn't teach the desired behaviour.)
MISCONCEPTIONS. "Psychology is just common sense/reading minds" → it's an empirical science. "Correlation = causation" → not necessarily. "Reinforcement = bribery always" → it's a systematic learning principle.
REVISION. Definition & goals; fields; methods & ethics; perspectives; biological basis; learning (classical/operant/observational).
ADAPTIVE — "reinforcement". L1: "A reinforcement is anything that makes a behaviour more likely to happen again." L2: "If a behaviour is followed by a reward (positive reinforcement) or by removing something unpleasant (negative reinforcement), it happens more often — like a star sticker making a child study more." L3: "Reinforcement is a consequence that increases the probability of the preceding operant response; positive adds a stimulus, negative removes an aversive one." L4: "Reinforcement is like watering a plant behaviour — the more you 'water' (reward) it, the more it grows." L5: "Apps use reinforcement — points, streaks, badges — to keep you coming back; it's operant conditioning in your pocket."
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain the difference between classical and operant conditioning."},{"role":"assistant","content":"CLASSICAL conditioning (Pavlov) is learning by ASSOCIATION: a neutral stimulus (a bell) is repeatedly paired with a natural one (food) until the bell alone triggers the response (salivation). The learner is passive — the association just forms. OPERANT conditioning (Skinner) is learning by CONSEQUENCES: a behaviour is strengthened by reinforcement (reward) or weakened by punishment. Here the learner is active — it does something and learns from what follows. Quick test: a dog salivating at a bell = classical; a dog sitting to earn a treat = operant."}]}

BRANCH A — NCERT / CBSE (Class 12 Commerce)

===== ACCOUNTANCY =====
Chapter arc: Accounting for Partnership — Basic Concepts · Reconstitution (Admission, Retirement/Death) · Dissolution of a Partnership Firm · Company Accounts — Share Capital & Debentures · Financial Statements of a Company · Analysis of Financial Statements · Accounting Ratios · Cash Flow Statement.

★ FLAGSHIP G12AC-PART — Partnership: Fundamentals & Profit & Loss Appropriation — FULL APPARATUS
1. METADATA
• Grade 12 · NCERT/CBSE · Commerce · Accountancy · Unit: Partnership · Chapter: Accounting for Partnership — Basic Concepts
• Prerequisites: Class 11 journal/ledger/final accounts; the accounting equation; capital accounts.
• Difficulty: Medium–Hard (foundation for all partnership chapters and a major board-marks block).
• Objectives: understand partnership features & the deed; distinguish fixed vs fluctuating capital accounts; compute interest on capital/drawings, partner's salary/commission; prepare the Profit & Loss Appropriation Account and partners' capital/current accounts; handle guarantee of profit and past adjustments.
2. TEACHING MATERIAL
(a) Meaning & features. A partnership is a relation between persons who agree to share the profits of a business carried on by all or any of them acting for all (Indian Partnership Act, 1932). Features: two or more persons, an agreement, lawful business, profit sharing, mutual agency, unlimited liability.
(b) Partnership deed. The written agreement stating capital, profit-sharing ratio, interest on capital/drawings, salaries, etc. In the absence of a deed, the Act applies: no interest on capital, no salary, no interest on drawings; profits shared equally; interest on partner's loan @ 6% p.a.
(c) Capital accounts — two methods.
 • Fluctuating capital: all adjustments (interest, salary, drawings, share of profit) pass through the single Capital Account; balance changes each year.
 • Fixed capital: Capital Account stays fixed (only fresh capital/withdrawal of capital); all other items go to a separate Current Account.
(d) Appropriation of profit. Net profit (from the P&L A/c) is distributed among partners through the Profit & Loss Appropriation Account (an extension of P&L). It is debited with interest on capital, salaries/commission to partners and the share of profit transferred to partners; it is credited with net profit b/d and interest on drawings.
 Order: charge interest on capital and salaries first; the remaining profit is shared in the profit-sharing ratio.
(e) Interest on capital & drawings.
 • Interest on capital = Capital × rate × time (an appropriation, allowed only if the deed permits, and only out of profits unless agreed otherwise).
 • Interest on drawings = Drawings × rate × time (charged to the partner). For even monthly drawings, use the average period: for equal amounts drawn at month-end for 12 months, average period = 5.5 months; beginning of month = 6.5; mid-month = 6.
(f) Partner's salary/commission. Only if the deed allows. Commission may be "on net profit before charging such commission" (rate/100 × profit) or "after charging it" (rate/(100+rate) × profit).
(g) Guarantee of profit. A partner may be guaranteed a minimum; any deficiency is borne by the guaranteeing partner(s) in the agreed ratio.
3. WORKED EXAMPLES (step-by-step)
Example 1 — P&L Appropriation. A and B are partners sharing profits 3:2. Capitals: A ₹2,00,000, B ₹1,00,000. Deed: interest on capital 10% p.a.; B's salary ₹2,000/month; net profit for the year ₹90,000.
Step 1 — Interest on capital: A = 2,00,000×10% = ₹20,000; B = 1,00,000×10% = ₹10,000 (total ₹30,000).
Step 2 — B's salary = 2,000×12 = ₹24,000.
Step 3 — Profit left for sharing = 90,000 − 30,000 − 24,000 = ₹36,000. Share: A = 36,000×3/5 = ₹21,600; B = 36,000×2/5 = ₹14,400.
P&L Appropriation A/c:
 Dr  |  ₹  |  Cr  |  ₹ 
------------------------------
 Interest on Capital: A 20,000; B 10,000  |  30,000  |  Net Profit b/d  |  90,000 
 Salary to B  |  24,000  |    |  Profit transferred: A 21,600; B 14,400  |  36,000  |    |  Total  |  90,000  |  Total  |  90,000 
Example 2 — Interest on drawings. C withdrew ₹5,000 at the end of every month; interest @ 6% p.a.
Total drawings = 5,000×12 = ₹60,000. Average period (end of month) = 5.5 months. Interest = 60,000 × 6% × 5.5/12 = ₹1,650. (Or: 5,000×6%×(11+10+…+0)/12 = 5,000×6%×66/12 = 1,650.)

Example 3 — No deed. X and Y start with capitals ₹3,00,000 and ₹1,00,000; no deed; profit ₹40,000. Since no deed: no interest on capital/salary; profit shared equally → X ₹20,000, Y ₹20,000 (capital ratio is irrelevant without a deed).

Example 4 — Guarantee. P, Q, R share 3:2:1; R is guaranteed minimum ₹30,000; profit ₹1,20,000. Normal shares: P 60,000, Q 40,000, R 20,000. R short by ₹10,000; deficiency borne by P & Q in 3:2 → P bears 6,000, Q bears 4,000. Final: P 54,000, Q 36,000, R 30,000.
4. VISUAL / DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION
A flow: Net Profit (P&L A/c) → P&L Appropriation A/c (Dr: interest on capital, salaries, profit share; Cr: net profit, interest on drawings) → Partners' Capital/Current Accounts.

A. DEFINITIONS (12)
1. Partnership. 2. Partnership deed. 3. Mutual agency. 4. Fixed capital account. 5. Fluctuating capital account. 6. Current account. 7. P&L Appropriation Account. 8. Interest on capital. 9. Interest on drawings. 10. Partner's salary/commission. 11. Profit-sharing ratio. 12. Guarantee of profit.

B. QUESTIONS & ANSWERS
Easy: Under the Act (no deed), what is the profit-sharing ratio? (Equal.) Interest on partner's loan without a deed? (6% p.a.) Medium: Where is a partner's salary recorded? (Debit of P&L Appropriation A/c.) Fixed vs fluctuating capital — key difference? (Adjustments via Current A/c vs Capital A/c.) Difficult: Compute interest on drawings of ₹2,000 drawn at the start of each month, 6% p.a. (2,000×12×6%×6.5/12 = ₹780.) Application: A guaranteed partner falls short by ₹6,000 — who bears it? (The guaranteeing partners in agreed ratio.) Reasoning: Why is interest on capital an appropriation, not a charge? (It's a distribution of profit, allowed only if the deed permits and profits exist.)

C. MCQs (20, abridged)
1. Without a deed, profits are shared equally. 2. Interest on partner's loan (no deed) = 6% p.a. 3. P&L Appropriation A/c distributes net profit. 4. Interest on capital is a(n) appropriation. 5. Salary to a partner is debited to P&L Appropriation. 6. Fixed capital uses a separate Current account. 7. Interest on drawings is credited to P&L Appropriation. 8. Mutual agency means each partner is an agent of the firm. 9. Average period for equal month-end drawings = 5.5 months. 10. Guarantee deficiency is borne by guaranteeing partners. 11. Partnership is governed by the Act of 1932. 12. Profit shared after interest & salary in the profit-sharing ratio. 13. Commission "before charging it" = rate/100 × profit. 14. Fluctuating capital changes every year. 15. Net profit is brought credit side of Appropriation A/c. 16. Interest on capital allowed only if the deed permits. 17. Drawings reduce a partner's capital/current balance. 18. Minimum number of partners = 2. 19. Interest on capital = capital × rate × time. 20. Salary to a partner is allowed only per the deed.

D. TRUE / FALSE (10)
1. Without a deed, interest on capital is allowed. F. 2. Partnership needs at least two persons. T. 3. Interest on drawings is debited to the appropriation account. F (credited). 4. Fixed capital accounts don't show the yearly profit share. T (it goes to current a/c). 5. A partner's salary is a charge against profit. F (appropriation). 6. Profit is shared equally if the deed is silent. T. 7. Interest on capital is an appropriation of profit. T. 8. Mutual agency binds partners to each other. T. 9. Guaranteed deficiency is borne by the guaranteed partner. F (by guarantors). 10. Interest on a partner's loan without a deed is 10%. F (6%).

E. FILL IN THE BLANKS (10)
1. Partnership Act year:  (1932). 2. Without a deed, profits shared  (equally). 3. Interest on capital = capital × rate ×  (time). 4. Salary to a partner is  to Appropriation A/c (debited). 5. Interest on drawings is  to Appropriation A/c (credited). 6. Fixed capital adjustments go to the  account (Current). 7. Loan interest without deed = % (6). 8. Net profit appears on the  side of Appropriation A/c (credit). 9. Deficiency in a guarantee is borne by the  partners (guaranteeing). 10. Minimum partners =  (2).

F. SHORT-ANSWER
• List any four provisions applied in the absence of a partnership deed.
• Differentiate fixed and fluctuating capital accounts.
• Compute interest on capital of ₹1,50,000 @ 8% for 9 months. (₹9,000.)

G. LONG-ANSWER (model, full solution)
Q: L and M are partners (3:2). Capitals ₹4,00,000 and ₹2,00,000. Deed: interest on capital 6% p.a.; M's salary ₹3,000/month; interest on drawings — L ₹1,200, M ₹800. Net profit before appropriations ₹1,00,000. Prepare the P&L Appropriation A/c and show profit shares.
Model: Interest on capital: L 24,000, M 12,000 (total 36,000). M's salary 36,000. Interest on drawings (credited): L 1,200 + M 800 = 2,000. Divisible profit = 1,00,000 + 2,000 − 36,000 − 36,000 = ₹30,000. Shares: L = 30,000×3/5 = 18,000; M = 30,000×2/5 = 12,000.
P&L Appropriation A/c: Dr — Interest on capital 36,000; Salary 36,000; Profit transferred (L 18,000, M 12,000) 30,000 = 1,02,000. Cr — Net profit 1,00,000; Interest on drawings 2,000 = 1,02,000. Totals ₹1,02,000 = ₹1,02,000 ✓.

H. HOTS
• If a firm's profit is insufficient to allow full interest on capital, how is interest allowed? (Proportionately, treating it as an appropriation — limited to available profit unless the deed makes it a charge.)
• Why can commission "after charging such commission" differ from "before"? (Different bases: rate/(100+rate) vs rate/100 of profit.)
• A partner draws unevenly through the year — which method computes interest on drawings? (Product method: sum of (amount × months) × rate/12.)

I. MISCONCEPTIONS
• "Profit is always shared in the capital ratio" → only if the deed says so; otherwise equally.
• "Interest on capital is always allowed" → only if the deed permits and (usually) out of profits.
• "Salary to a partner is an expense (charge)" → it's an appropriation of profit unless the deed makes it a charge.
• "Interest on drawings reduces the appropriation account's debit" → it's credited (increases divisible profit).

J. REVISION
Key points: partnership features & deed; no-deed provisions (equal profits, no interest/salary, 6% loan interest); fixed vs fluctuating capital; P&L Appropriation A/c (Dr: interest on capital, salary, profit share; Cr: net profit, interest on drawings); interest on capital/drawings; guarantee.
Formula sheet: Interest on capital = C×r×t; Interest on drawings (equal, month-end) = Total×r×5.5/12; commission (before) = r/100×P, (after) = r/(100+r)×P.
Vocabulary: deed, appropriation, fixed/fluctuating capital, current account, guarantee, profit-sharing ratio.

ADAPTIVE — Concept: "P&L Appropriation Account"
• L1 Beginner: "It's the account that shares out a partnership's profit among the partners."
• L2 Grade level: "After the P&L Account finds net profit, the Appropriation Account distributes it — first paying interest on capital and any partner salaries, then splitting what's left in the profit-sharing ratio."
• L3 Technical: "The P&L Appropriation Account is an extension of the P&L Account that allocates net profit to partners via interest on capital, remuneration and residual profit, while crediting interest on drawings, per the partnership deed."
• L4 Analogy: "Think of net profit as a cake: the Appropriation Account first cuts fixed slices (interest on capital, salaries) that the deed promises, then divides the remaining cake in each partner's agreed share."
• L5 Real-world: "Any real partnership firm — a law firm, a shop run by two friends — uses this to fairly settle who gets what at year-end, honouring who invested more or worked more."

The P&L Appropriation Account does this. On its DEBIT side go the 'appropriations': interest on partners' capital, partners' salaries/commission, and finally each partner's share of the remaining profit. On its CREDIT side go net profit brought down and interest charged on partners' drawings. The two sides balance, and each partner's share flows into their Capital (or Current) Account."}]}

★ CHAPTER G12AC-RATIO — Accounting Ratios — MEDIUM ANCHOR (numerical)
METADATA. Grade 12 · NCERT · Accountancy · Unit: Analysis of financial statements · Prereq: financial statements · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: compute and interpret liquidity, solvency, activity and profitability ratios.
TEACHING. Ratio analysis expresses relationships between financial figures to judge performance.
 • Liquidity: Current Ratio = Current Assets / Current Liabilities (ideal ≈ 2:1); Quick Ratio = (CA − Stock − Prepaid)/CL (ideal ≈ 1:1).
 • Solvency: Debt–Equity = Debt/Equity; Total Assets to Debt; Interest Coverage.
 • Activity/Turnover: Inventory Turnover = COGS/Avg Stock; Debtors Turnover = Net Credit Sales/Avg Debtors; Working Capital Turnover.
 • Profitability: Gross Profit Ratio = (GP/Sales)×100; Net Profit Ratio; Operating Ratio; Return on Investment.
Worked (step-by-step): Current Assets ₹4,00,000 (incl. stock ₹1,00,000), Current Liabilities ₹2,00,000. Current Ratio = 4,00,000/2,00,000 = 2:1. Quick Ratio = (4,00,000−1,00,000)/2,00,000 = 1.5:1.
DEFINITIONS (6). ratio analysis, current ratio, quick ratio, debt-equity ratio, inventory turnover, gross profit ratio.
Q&A/MCQ (condensed). Ideal current ratio: 2:1; quick ratio excludes stock & prepaid; GP ratio = (GP/Sales)×100; high debt-equity signals more risk; inventory turnover uses COGS/avg stock.
HOTS. A firm has a high current ratio but low quick ratio — what does that imply? (Large stock holdings; possible liquidity concern.)
ADAPTIVE — "current ratio". L1: "It checks if a business can pay its short-term bills." L2: "Current ratio = current assets ÷ current liabilities; 2:1 means ₹2 of short-term assets for every ₹1 owed soon." L3: "A liquidity metric measuring short-term solvency; ratios well below/above the norm signal illiquidity/idle assets." L4: "Like checking if your wallet plus this month's income covers this month's bills — with a comfortable cushion." L5: "Lenders look at it before giving a firm a short-term loan, to be sure it can repay."
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Calculate current and quick ratio: current assets ₹4,00,000 including stock ₹1,00,000, current liabilities ₹2,00,000."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Current Ratio = Current Assets ÷ Current Liabilities = 4,00,000 ÷ 2,00,000 = 2 : 1 (the ideal benchmark).\nQuick Ratio = (Current Assets − Stock) ÷ Current Liabilities = (4,00,000 − 1,00,000) ÷ 2,00,000 = 3,00,000 ÷ 2,00,000 = 1.5 : 1 (comfortably above the 1:1 norm). The firm looks liquid."}]}

===== ECONOMICS =====

★ CHAPTER G12EC-NI — National Income & its Measurement (Macroeconomics) — MEDIUM ANCHOR
METADATA. Grade 12 · NCERT · Economics · Unit: Introductory Macroeconomics · Prereq: basic micro, aggregates · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: define macro aggregates (GDP, GNP, NNP, NI); the three methods of measurement; distinguish nominal vs real GDP.
TEACHING. Macroeconomics studies the economy as a whole (output, income, employment, price level). National income = the total value of final goods and services produced in a year.
 • GDP (Gross Domestic Product) — value of final output produced within a country's borders. GNP = GDP + net factor income from abroad (NFIA). NNP = GNP − depreciation. National Income (NNP at factor cost) = NNP at market price − net indirect taxes.
 • Three measurement methods (all give the same total): Value-added (product) method (sum of value added, avoiding double counting), Income method (wages + rent + interest + profit = factor incomes), Expenditure method (C + I + G + (X − M)).
 • Real vs Nominal GDP: nominal uses current prices; real GDP uses base-year prices (removes inflation). GDP deflator = (Nominal/Real)×100.
Worked (step-by-step): Given C=500, I=200, G=150, X=80, M=50 → GDP (expenditure) = 500+200+150+(80−50) = ₹880. If NFIA = +20, GNP = 900; if depreciation = 40, NNP = 860; if net indirect taxes = 30, NI (NNP at FC) = ₹830.
DEFINITIONS (8). macroeconomics, GDP, GNP, NNP, national income, value added, real GDP, GDP deflator.
Q&A/MCQ (condensed). GDP counts final goods; GNP = GDP + NFIA; expenditure method: C+I+G+(X−M); real GDP uses base-year prices; NNP = GNP − depreciation; three methods give the same total.
HOTS. Why avoid double counting in the product method? (Intermediate goods' value is already in final goods.) Can nominal GDP rise while real GDP falls? (Yes — if prices rise faster than output.)
ADAPTIVE — "GDP". L1: "GDP is the total value of everything a country produces in a year." L2: "GDP adds up all FINAL goods and services made within a country's borders in a year — a measure of its economic size." L3: "GDP is the market value of final output within domestic territory over a period, computable by product, income or expenditure approaches which coincide by the circular-flow identity." L4: "GDP is like a country's annual 'total sales receipt' — how much new value everyone together created." L5: "News says 'GDP grew 7%' — that's real output rising, signalling more jobs, income and goods for people."
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Compute GDP by the expenditure method: C=500, I=200, G=150, exports=80, imports=50."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Expenditure method: GDP = C + I + G + (X − M).\n= 500 + 200 + 150 + (80 − 50)\n= 500 + 200 + 150 + 30\n= ₹880.\nThe (X − M) term is NET exports; imports are subtracted because they're produced abroad, not domestically."}]}

===== BUSINESS STUDIES =====

★ CHAPTER G12BS-MGMT — Management: Nature, Principles & Functions — MEDIUM ANCHOR
METADATA. Grade 12 · NCERT · Business Studies · Unit: Principles & functions of management · Prereq: forms of business · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: define management; its nature & importance; Taylor's scientific & Fayol's principles; the functions of management.
TEACHING. Management is the process of getting things done through others to achieve goals effectively (right tasks) and efficiently (least resources). Nature: goal-oriented, all-pervasive, multidimensional, continuous, a group activity; both a science and an art, and an evolving profession. Functions: Planning (deciding in advance), Organising (arranging resources/authority), Staffing (people), Directing (leading, motivating, communicating), Controlling (measuring & correcting). Fayol's 14 principles (e.g., division of work, unity of command, scalar chain). Taylor's scientific management (science not rule of thumb, harmony, cooperation, time-and-motion study).
DEFINITIONS (8). management, effectiveness, efficiency, planning, organising, staffing, directing, controlling.
Q&A/MCQ (condensed). First function of management: planning; unity of command means one boss; measuring & correcting performance: controlling; Taylor is the father of scientific management; management balances effectiveness & efficiency; Fayol gave 14 principles.
HOTS. Can a firm be efficient but not effective? (Yes — using few resources but missing the goal.) Why is planning called the primary function? (All other functions flow from plans.)
ADAPTIVE — "management". L1: "Management is getting work done through people to reach goals." L2: "It's coordinating people and resources to achieve aims both effectively (doing the right things) and efficiently (with least waste)." L3: "Management is the process of planning, organising, staffing, directing and controlling organisational resources toward objectives under uncertainty." L4: "A manager is like an orchestra conductor — not playing every instrument, but coordinating many players so they produce harmony (results)." L5: "Whether running a shop, a hospital or a cricket team, the same five functions — plan, organise, staff, direct, control — keep it working."
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"What is management and what are its functions?"},{"role":"assistant","content":"Management is the process of getting organisational goals achieved through others — doing so EFFECTIVELY (achieving the right results) and EFFICIENTLY (using the least resources). Its five functions are: PLANNING (deciding goals and how to reach them), ORGANISING (arranging resources and authority), STAFFING (getting and keeping the right people), DIRECTING (leading, motivating and communicating), and CONTROLLING (checking performance against plans and correcting deviations). Planning comes first because every other function builds on it."}]}

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 12  |  NCERT  |  Economics  |  CHAPTER: National Income  |  CONCEPT: Macro aggregates & measurement
EXPLANATION: GDP/GNP/NNP/NI; product/income/expenditure methods (equal totals); nominal vs real GDP & deflator.  |  EXAMPLE: C+I+G+(X−M) = 880  |  APPLICATION: Judging economic growth  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Count intermediate goods  |  QUESTION: GNP = GDP + ?  |  ANSWER: NFIA  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 12  |  NCERT  |  Business Studies  |  CHAPTER: Management  |  CONCEPT: Nature, principles, functions
EXPLANATION: Effectiveness vs efficiency; five functions (planning→controlling); Taylor & Fayol.  |  EXAMPLE: Controlling = measure & correct  |  APPLICATION: Running any organisation  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Efficient always means effective  |  QUESTION: First function of management?  |  ANSWER: Planning  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

BRANCH A — NCERT / CBSE (Class 11 Commerce)

===== ACCOUNTANCY =====
Chapter arc: Introduction to Accounting · Theory Base of Accounting · Recording of Transactions I (Journal) · Recording of Transactions II (Ledger, Cash Book, Subsidiary Books) · Bank Reconciliation Statement · Trial Balance & Rectification of Errors · Depreciation, Provisions & Reserves · Bills of Exchange · Financial Statements I & II.

★ FLAGSHIP G11AC-CYCLE — Double-Entry System & the Accounting Cycle — FULL APPARATUS
1. METADATA
• Grade 11 · NCERT/CBSE · Stream Commerce · Subject Accountancy · Unit: Recording of transactions
• Prerequisites: basic arithmetic; idea of business, money, buying/selling.
• Difficulty: Medium (the foundation of ALL later accounting).
• Objectives: understand accounting terms & the accounting equation; apply the rules of debit and credit under both approaches; pass journal entries; post to ledgers; balance accounts; prepare a trial balance — all with correct format and step-by-step working.
2. TEACHING MATERIAL
(a) What accounting is. Accounting is the process of identifying, recording, classifying, summarising and communicating financial transactions of a business. Bookkeeping (recording) is one part of it.
(b) Key terms. Business transaction (a measurable financial event); account (a summary record of a particular item); capital (owner's investment); drawings (owner's withdrawals); assets (what the business owns), liabilities (what it owes), revenue/income, expenses; debtor (owes us), creditor (we owe them); goods, purchases, sales.
(c) The accounting equation (the backbone).
  Assets = Liabilities + Capital
 Every transaction keeps this equation balanced. Expanded: Capital = Capital introduced + Profits − Drawings − Losses. Example: owner invests ₹1,00,000 cash → Assets (Cash) ₹1,00,000 = Capital ₹1,00,000. Buys furniture ₹20,000 cash → Cash −20,000, Furniture +20,000 (assets reshuffle; equation still balances).
(d) Double-entry system. Every transaction affects at least two accounts — one debited (Dr), one credited (Cr) — for equal amounts, so total debits always equal total credits. This dual aspect is why books "balance."
(e) Rules of debit and credit.
 Modern (accounting-equation) approach:
 Account type  |  Debit (increase/decrease)  |  Credit 
------------------------------
 Assets  |  Increase → Dr  |  Decrease → Cr 
 Expenses/Losses  |  Increase → Dr  |  Decrease → Cr 
 Liabilities  |  Decrease → Dr  |  Increase → Cr 
 Capital  |  Decrease → Dr  |  Increase → Cr 
 Revenue/Income  |  Decrease → Dr  |  Increase → Cr 
 Traditional (British) approach: Personal accounts — "Debit the receiver, credit the giver"; Real accounts — "Debit what comes in, credit what goes out"; Nominal accounts — "Debit all expenses/losses, credit all incomes/gains."
(f) Journal. The book of original entry. Each entry: date, accounts (Dr first, then Cr with "To"), amounts in Dr/Cr columns, and a narration (brief explanation).
(g) Ledger. Accounts are posted from the journal to individual ledger accounts (T-format: debit side left, credit side right), then balanced (difference = balance c/d).
(h) Trial balance. A list of all ledger balances (debit balances in one column, credit in another). If double entry is correct, total debits = total credits — an arithmetical check (not a proof of no errors).
3. WORKED EXAMPLES (step-by-step)
Example 1 — Journal entries. Record for M/s Ravi Traders:
(i) Started business with cash ₹50,000. (ii) Bought goods for cash ₹10,000. (iii) Sold goods for cash ₹6,000. (iv) Bought furniture on credit from Mohan ₹8,000. (v) Paid rent ₹2,000.
 Date  |  Particulars  |  L.F.  |  Dr (₹)  |  Cr (₹) 
------------------------------
 (i)  |  Cash A/c ................ Dr  |    |  50,000  |    |  &nbsp;&nbsp;To Capital A/c  |    |    |  50,000  |  (Started business with cash)  |    |    |  (ii)  |  Purchases A/c ......... Dr  |    |  10,000  |    |  &nbsp;&nbsp;To Cash A/c  |    |    |  10,000  |  (Goods bought for cash)  |    |    |  (iii)  |  Cash A/c ................ Dr  |    |  6,000  |    |  &nbsp;&nbsp;To Sales A/c  |    |    |  6,000  |  (Goods sold for cash)  |    |    |  (iv)  |  Furniture A/c ......... Dr  |    |  8,000  |    |  &nbsp;&nbsp;To Mohan A/c  |    |    |  8,000  |  (Furniture bought on credit)  |    |    |  (v)  |  Rent A/c ................ Dr  |    |  2,000  |    |  &nbsp;&nbsp;To Cash A/c  |    |    |  2,000  |  (Rent paid)  |    |    |  Reasoning per entry: (i) Cash (asset) ↑ Dr; Capital ↑ Cr. (ii) Purchases (expense) ↑ Dr; Cash ↓ Cr. (iii) Cash ↑ Dr; Sales (income) ↑ Cr. (iv) Furniture (asset) ↑ Dr; Mohan (creditor/liability) ↑ Cr. (v) Rent (expense) ↑ Dr; Cash ↓ Cr.

Example 2 — Ledger (Cash A/c). Post the cash transactions above:
Dr side: To Capital 50,000; To Sales 6,000. Cr side: By Purchases 10,000; By Rent 2,000; By Balance c/d 44,000. Totals 56,000 = 56,000. Closing cash balance = ₹44,000 (Dr).

Example 3 — Trial balance (extract).
 Account  |  Dr (₹)  |  Cr (₹) 
------------------------------
 Cash  |  44,000  |  Purchases  |  10,000  |  Furniture  |  8,000  |  Rent  |  2,000  |  Capital  |    |  50,000 
 Sales  |    |  6,000 
 Mohan (creditor)  |    |  8,000 
 Total  |  64,000  |  64,000 
Debits = Credits ⇒ arithmetically correct.

Example 4 — Accounting equation. Show the effect: (a) commenced with cash ₹1,00,000; (b) bought goods ₹30,000 cash; (c) sold half the goods for ₹25,000 cash.
(a) Assets: Cash 1,00,000 = Capital 1,00,000.
(b) Cash 70,000 + Stock 30,000 = Capital 1,00,000.
(c) Cash 95,000 + Stock 15,000 = Capital 1,00,000 + Profit 10,000 → Assets 1,10,000 = Capital 1,10,000. (Profit = 25,000 − 15,000 cost.)
4. VISUAL / DIAGRAM DESCRIPTION
A flow arrow: Transaction → Journal (with narration) → Ledger (T-accounts, balanced) → Trial Balance → Financial Statements. Beside it, a T-account skeleton labelled Dr (left)  |  Cr (right).

A. DEFINITIONS (12)
1. Accounting. 2. Transaction. 3. Account. 4. Debit / Credit. 5. Double-entry system. 6. Accounting equation. 7. Asset. 8. Liability. 9. Capital. 10. Journal. 11. Ledger. 12. Trial balance.

B. QUESTIONS & ANSWERS
Easy: State the accounting equation. (Assets = Liabilities + Capital.) What is a debtor? (One who owes the business.) Medium: Which account is debited when goods are bought for cash? (Purchases.) On what side is an increase in a liability recorded? (Credit.) Difficult: Journalise: paid salary ₹5,000 by cheque. (Salary A/c Dr 5,000 / To Bank A/c 5,000.) Application: Owner withdraws ₹3,000 cash for personal use — entry? (Drawings A/c Dr 3,000 / To Cash A/c 3,000.) Reasoning: Why do total debits equal total credits? (Every transaction has equal dual aspects — double entry.)

C. MCQs (20, abridged)
1. Accounting equation: Assets = Liabilities + Capital. 2. Every transaction affects at least two accounts. 3. Increase in asset is a debit. 4. Increase in capital is a credit. 5. Book of original entry: journal. 6. Posting is done to the ledger. 7. Trial balance checks arithmetical accuracy. 8. Drawings reduce capital. 9. Rent paid is a(n) expense (Dr). 10. Amount owed to a supplier is a liability. 11. "Debit the receiver" applies to personal accounts. 12. Purchases A/c is a nominal account. 13. Sales increase is a credit. 14. A creditor is on the credit side. 15. Machinery is a(n) asset. 16. Capital A/c has a normal credit balance. 17. Narration is written below each journal entry. 18. Cash A/c is a real account. 19. Decrease in liability is a debit. 20. Trial balance totals must be equal.

D. TRUE / FALSE (10)
1. Assets = Liabilities + Capital. T. 2. Only one account is affected per transaction. F (at least two). 3. Increase in an asset is credited. F (debited). 4. Journal is the book of original entry. T. 5. A trial balance proves there are no errors. F (only arithmetical check). 6. Drawings increase capital. F (decrease). 7. Rent is a nominal (expense) account. T. 8. Capital normally has a debit balance. F (credit). 9. Posting means transferring entries to the ledger. T. 10. Total debits equal total credits in double entry. T.

E. FILL IN THE BLANKS (10)
1. Assets = Liabilities +  (Capital). 2. Book of original entry is the  (journal). 3. Increase in a liability is a  (credit). 4. Owner's withdrawals are called  (drawings). 5. The T-account has debit on the  side (left). 6. Posting transfers entries to the  (ledger). 7. A  balance lists all ledger balances (trial). 8. "Debit what comes in" applies to  accounts (real). 9. Purchase of goods for cash debits  (Purchases). 10. Capital has a normal  balance (credit).

F. SHORT-ANSWER
• State the modern rules of debit and credit for assets and liabilities.
• Journalise: sold goods to Ramesh on credit ₹4,000. (Ramesh Dr / To Sales 4,000.)
• Distinguish between a debtor and a creditor.

G. LONG-ANSWER (model, with full solution)
Q: From the following of Sneha Traders, pass journal entries, post the Cash A/c, and prepare a trial balance: (1) Started business with cash ₹80,000. (2) Purchased goods for cash ₹25,000. (3) Sold goods for cash ₹18,000. (4) Purchased goods on credit from Anil ₹12,000. (5) Paid wages ₹3,000.
Model:
Journal — (1) Cash Dr 80,000 / To Capital 80,000. (2) Purchases Dr 25,000 / To Cash 25,000. (3) Cash Dr 18,000 / To Sales 18,000. (4) Purchases Dr 12,000 / To Anil 12,000. (5) Wages Dr 3,000 / To Cash 3,000.
Cash A/c — Dr: To Capital 80,000; To Sales 18,000 = 98,000. Cr: By Purchases 25,000; By Wages 3,000; By Balance c/d 70,000 = 98,000. Cash balance ₹70,000 (Dr).
Trial balance — Dr: Cash 70,000, Purchases 37,000, Wages 3,000 = 1,10,000. Cr: Capital 80,000, Sales 18,000, Anil 12,000 = 1,10,000. Totals ₹1,10,000 = ₹1,10,000 ✓.

H. HOTS
• A trial balance tallies, yet the accountant has recorded a purchase as a sale. Will the trial balance reveal it? (No — it's an error of principle; totals still match.)
• If capital is ₹2,00,000 and liabilities ₹50,000, what are total assets? (₹2,50,000.)
• The owner brings in a personal laptop worth ₹40,000 for business use — is it a transaction, and what's the entry? (Yes; Computer/Laptop A/c Dr 40,000 / To Capital 40,000.)

I. MISCONCEPTIONS
• "Debit means decrease and credit means increase" → depends on account type (asset up = debit).
• "A tallied trial balance means no errors" → some errors (omission, principle, compensating) don't affect the tally.
• "Drawings are a business expense" → they reduce capital, not profit.
• "Capital is an asset of the business" → it is a liability of the business to its owner.

J. REVISION
Key points: accounting equation (A = L + C); double entry (equal Dr & Cr); modern & traditional rules; journal→ledger→trial balance cycle; narration; trial balance is an arithmetical check only.
Format sheet: Journal (Date  |  Particulars (Dr…/To…)  |  LF  |  Dr  |  Cr  |  Narration); Ledger T-account (Dr  |  Cr, balance c/d); Trial balance (Dr column = Cr column).
Vocabulary: debit, credit, asset, liability, capital, drawings, debtor, creditor, ledger, trial balance.

ADAPTIVE — Concept: "double-entry / debit-credit"
• L1 Beginner: "Every money event in a business is written twice — once as a debit and once as a credit — for the same amount."
• L2 Grade level: "For each transaction, one account is debited and another credited by equal amounts. E.g., paying rent in cash: Rent (expense) debited, Cash (asset) credited by ₹2,000."
• L3 Technical: "The double-entry system records the dual aspect of each transaction to preserve the accounting identity Assets = Liabilities + Capital; debits equal credits by construction."
• L4 Analogy: "Every transaction is a two-sided coin — value flows FROM one account (credit) TO another (debit); the coin's two faces always sum to the same amount, keeping the books balanced like a see-saw."
• L5 Real-world: "When you buy a phone on EMI, your accounts show an asset gained (phone, debit) and a liability owed (loan, credit) — the same logic every business uses to track its money."

STEP: compute annual dep, prepare asset A/c year-wise.  |  QUESTION: SLM dep on ₹1,00,000 asset, ₹10,000 scrap, 9 years? ANSWER: (100000−10000)/9 = ₹10,000/year.  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium
