CHAPTER G3M-A — Multiplication ("Give and Take" / tables) — FULL APPARATUS
METADATA. Grade 3 · NCERT · Maths · Unit: Operations · Prereq: skip-counting, addition, arrays (G2) · Difficulty: Medium · Objectives: understand multiplication as repeated addition and as arrays; build and use tables (2–10); multiply a 2-digit number by a 1-digit number with and without regrouping; know properties (order/commutativity, ×1, ×0, ×10); solve word problems.

TEACHING MATERIAL.
Introduction. Multiplication is a fast way to add equal groups. Instead of 6+6+6+6, we say "4 groups of 6" = 4 × 6.
Meaning 1 — repeated addition. 4 × 6 means six added four times → 24. The first number = how many groups; the second = size of each group.
Meaning 2 — arrays. A rectangle of dots in 4 rows and 6 columns has 4 × 6 = 24 dots. Turning the array sideways (6 rows, 4 columns) still gives 24 — this shows order does not matter (commutative property): 4 × 6 = 6 × 4.
Meaning 3 — number line jumps. Start at 0 and jump 6 four times → lands on 24.
Key facts (properties). ×1 leaves a number unchanged (7×1=7); ×0 gives 0 (7×0=0); ×10 puts a 0 at the end (7×10=70); multiplying by 100 puts two zeros.
Tables. Each table is repeated addition of that number. Patterns help memory: the 5-table ends in 5 or 0; the 9-table digits add to 9 (9,18,27… → 9,9,9); the 10-table ends in 0.
2-digit × 1-digit (column method). Multiply ones, then tens, carrying when a product ≥10. Example 23 × 4: ones 3×4=12 → write 2 carry 1; tens 2×4=8, +1 = 9 → 92. With regrouping 27 × 6: 7×6=42 → 2 carry 4; 2×6=12, +4=16 → 162.

Worked examples.
1. 8 × 7 = 56 (memorise; check: 8×7 = 8×5 + 8×2 = 40+16 = 56).
2. 34 × 3: 4×3=12 (2 carry 1); 3×3=9, +1=10 → 102.
3. Word: "A box has 12 pencils. How many in 5 boxes?" → 12 × 5 = 60 pencils.

Visual/diagram description. A 4×6 grid of dots labelled "4 rows, 6 in each = 24"; beside it the same grid rotated to "6 rows, 4 in each = 24" to show commutativity.

DEFINITIONS (10). 1. Multiplication — repeated addition of equal groups. 2. Factor — a number being multiplied. 3. Product — the result. 4. Multiplicand/multiplier — the number multiplied / doing the multiplying. 5. Array — rows-and-columns arrangement. 6. Times table — list of a number's multiples. 7. Multiple — a product of a number and a whole number. 8. Commutative property — order doesn't change the product. 9. Regrouping/carrying — moving a ten during multiplication. 10. Identity (×1) / zero (×0) properties.

Q&A. Easy: What is 6×3? (18.) 9×1? (9.) 5×0? (0.) Medium: Write 7+7+7+7 as a multiplication (4×7=28). Is 3×8 the same as 8×3? (Yes, 24.) Difficult: 46×5? (230.) 38×7? (266.) Application: 6 rows of 8 chairs — how many chairs? (48.) A pencil costs ₹4; cost of 15? (₹60.) Reasoning: Why does 7×10 end in 0? (Multiplying by ten shifts every place up, adding a zero.)

MCQs (12, abridged). 4×6=24; 9×0=0; 8×1=8; 5×7=35; 12×3=36; 25×4=100; 7×10=70; commutative: 3×9=9×3; 6+6+6=3×6; 9-table digit sum of 27=9; array 5×4 dots=20; 23×4=92.

T/F (10). 4×6=6×4 T. 8×0=8 F (=0). Multiplying by 1 changes the number F. 7×10=70 T. 3×5 means 5 groups of 3 or 3 groups of 5 (same product) T. 12×5=50 F (=60). Every multiple of 5 ends in 5 or 0 T. An array shows multiplication T. 9×9=81 T. Order matters in multiplication F.

FILL BLANKS (10). 6×=42 (7). 8×5= (40). ×0=0 (any). 10×9= (90). 4×=24 (6). 7×7= (49). 3×=27 (9). 12×4= (48). ×1=15 (15). 5×6=6× (5).

SHORT-ANSWER. State the commutative property with an example. Compute 27×6. Write the 4-table up to ×10.

LONG-ANSWER (model). Q: Explain two ways to find 6 × 7 and check they agree. Model: (i) Repeated addition: 7+7+7+7+7+7 = 42. (ii) Break apart: 6×7 = 6×5 + 6×2 = 30+12 = 42. Both give 42, confirming the product.

HOTS. If 8×6=48, use it to find 8×7 without a table. (48+8=56.) A shop stacks apples in 9 rows of 9; is that more or fewer than 80? (81 — one more than 80.) Find a number that gives the same result whether you add it to itself or multiply it by 2 (any number — a+a = 2×a).

MISCONCEPTIONS. "×0 gives the number" → it gives 0. "3×4 and 3+4 are close" → 12 vs 7; different operations. "Order changes the product" → it doesn't (commutative). "Carrying is optional" → the carried ten must be added to the next column.

REVISION. Multiplication = repeated addition = arrays; commutative; ×0=0, ×1=same, ×10 adds a zero; column method with carrying; tables 2–10 patterns.
Formula/fact sheet: a×b = b×a; a×1=a; a×0=0; a×10 → append 0.

ADAPTIVE — "multiplication". L1: "Multiplication is a quick way to add the same number many times." L2: "4×6 means four groups of six; add 6 four times to get 24, or picture 4 rows of 6 dots." L3: "Multiplication is a binary operation on whole numbers giving the cardinality of a×b arrays; it is commutative, associative, and distributes over addition." L4: "Like stamping the same shape in a grid — 4 rows, 6 stamps each — you count all stamps at once instead of one by one." L5: "At a party, 6 tables each seat 8 people — 6×8=48 guests, found instantly instead of counting every chair."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.
``json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain multiplication to a Grade 3 student."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Multiplication is fast adding of equal groups! '4 × 6' means 4 groups of 6. You could add 6+6+6+6 = 24, OR picture 4 rows with 6 dots each — count them all: 24. A cool secret: the order doesn't matter, so 4×6 = 6×4. Want to try 5×7 both ways?"}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Solve 27 × 6 step by step."},{"role":"assistant","content":"  27\n×  6\nStep 1 (ones): 7×6 = 42. Write 2, carry 4.\nStep 2 (tens): 2×6 = 12, add the carried 4 → 16. Write 16.\nAnswer: 162. Check: 27×6 = (25×6)+(2×6) = 150+12 = 162 ✓"}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Why does multiplying by 10 add a zero?"},{"role":"assistant","content":"Because multiplying by 10 pushes every digit up one place — ones become tens, tens become hundreds — and the ones place is left empty, which we fill with 0. So 7×10 = 70, 34×10 = 340. It's the place-value system doing the work!"}]}

CHAPTER G3M-B — Division & Fair Sharing ("Fair Share") — FULL APPARATUS
METADATA. Grade 3 · NCERT · Maths · Prereq: multiplication · Medium · Objectives: understand division as equal sharing and as equal grouping; relate division to multiplication (inverse); divide within tables; understand remainder; introduce the unit-fraction idea of a "share."

TEACHING MATERIAL.
Two meanings. (i) Sharing: 12 sweets shared equally among 4 children → 3 each (12 ÷ 4 = 3). (ii) Grouping: 12 sweets put into groups of 4 → 3 groups (12 ÷ 4 = 3). Both give the same answer.
Division undoes multiplication. Because 4 × 3 = 12, we have 12 ÷ 4 = 3 and 12 ÷ 3 = 4. Every "fact family" links two multiplications and two divisions.
Remainder. Not every number shares evenly. 13 ÷ 4 = 3 with remainder 1 (3 each, 1 left over). The remainder is always smaller than the divisor.
Terms. dividend ÷ divisor = quotient (remainder). In 13 ÷ 4: dividend 13, divisor 4, quotient 3, remainder 1.
Fraction seed. Sharing 1 chapati between 2 = one-half (½) each — the beginning of fractions.

Worked examples.
1. 20 ÷ 5 = 4 (5×4=20). 2. 17 ÷ 3 = 5 r 2 (3×5=15, 17−15=2). 3. Word: "24 laddoos, 6 boxes, equal — how many per box?" → 24 ÷ 6 = 4.

Visual/diagram description. 12 circles being dealt one-by-one into 4 boxes until each box holds 3 — showing equal sharing.

DEFINITIONS (10). division, share equally, group equally, dividend, divisor, quotient, remainder, inverse operation, fact family, unit fraction (½, ⅓).

Q&A. Easy: 15 ÷ 3? (5.) 10 ÷ 2? (5.) Medium: 19 ÷ 4? (4 r 3.) Write the fact family of 6, 4, 24. (4×6=24, 6×4=24, 24÷4=6, 24÷6=4.) Application: 28 students in 4 equal rows — per row? (7.) Share 25 pens among 5 → each? (5.) Reasoning: Why can't the remainder be 4 when dividing by 4? (It would make another full group.)

MCQs (12, abridged). 20÷4=5; 18÷3=6; 13÷4=3 r 1; 24÷6=4; inverse of ×: ÷; remainder of 22÷5=2; 30÷5=6; 0÷7=0; 9÷1=9; share 12/3=4; dividend in 15÷5: 15; quotient of 35÷7: 5.

T/F (10). Division is repeated subtraction of equal groups T. 12÷4 and 4÷12 are equal F. Remainder can equal the divisor F. 24÷6=4 because 6×4=24 T. 0÷5=0 T. 5÷0 has a normal answer F (undefined). Sharing and grouping give different answers F. Quotient is the answer of division T. Every remainder is less than the divisor T. 9÷1=9 T.

FILL BLANKS (10). 16÷=4 (4). 21÷3= (7). 13÷4=3 r  (1). 24÷=6 (4). ÷5=6 (30). 0÷9= (0). 18÷2= (9). fact family of 3,7,21: 21÷7= (3). 45÷9= (5). remainder of 20÷6= (2).

SHORT/LONG. Short: Divide 29 by 6, state quotient and remainder. Long (model): "Explain, with sharing and grouping, why 15 ÷ 3 = 5." Model — Sharing: deal 15 among 3 people → 5 each. Grouping: make groups of 3 from 15 → 5 groups. Same answer 5; and 3×5=15 confirms it.

HOTS. Find a number that leaves remainder 2 when divided by 5 and remainder 0 when divided by 3 (e.g., 12: 12÷5=2 r2, 12÷3=4 r0). Why is 7÷0 impossible? (You can't make groups of nothing; nothing shared can total 7.)

MISCONCEPTIONS. "Division is commutative" → it is not (12÷4≠4÷12). "Remainder can be any size" → it must be < divisor. "Dividing by 0 gives 0" → it is undefined.

REVISION. Two meanings (share/group); division inverts multiplication; fact families; remainder < divisor; ÷1 unchanged, ÷ itself =1, 0÷n=0, n÷0 undefined.

ADAPTIVE — "remainder". L1: "The remainder is what's left over when things can't be shared equally." L2: "Share 13 toffees among 4 kids: each gets 3 (that's 12) and 1 toffee is left — the remainder is 1." L3: "In Euclidean division a = bq + r with 0 ≤ r < b; r is the residue." L4: "Like seating people at tables of 4 — if one extra person arrives, they're the 'leftover' who can't fill a new table alone." L5: "Packing 22 eggs into cartons of 6: you fill 3 cartons and 4 eggs remain uncartoned — remainder 4."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain division with a remainder to a child."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Sometimes things don't share out evenly! Say 13 chocolates among 4 friends. Give one to each, again, again — each friend gets 3 (that's 12 chocolates), and 1 chocolate is LEFT OVER. That leftover is the remainder. So 13 ÷ 4 = 3 remainder 1. The remainder is always smaller than the number of friends."}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"How is division connected to multiplication?"},{"role":"assistant","content":"They're opposites — they undo each other! Since 4 × 6 = 24, we instantly know 24 ÷ 4 = 6 and 24 ÷ 6 = 4. This is a 'fact family': the three numbers 4, 6, 24 give two multiplications and two divisions. Knowing your tables makes division much easier!"}]}

SUBJECT: EVS — Our Wondrous World (Class 3)
Verified units: 1 Our Families and Communities (Family and Friends; Going to the Mela; Celebrating Festivals); 2 Life Around Us (Getting to Know Plants; Plants and Animals Live Together; Living in Harmony); 3 Gifts of Nature (Water—A Precious Gift; Food We Eat; Staying Healthy and Happy); 4 Things Around Us (This World of Things; Making Things; Taking Charge of Waste).

CHAPTER G3E-A — Getting to Know Plants — FULL APPARATUS (anchor)
METADATA. Grade 3 · NCERT · EVS · Unit: Life Around Us · Prereq: Grade 1–2 plant awareness · Easy–Medium · Objectives: name plant parts and their functions; classify plants (trees/shrubs/herbs/climbers/creepers); understand what plants need; know plants as food/oxygen sources; describe seed→plant growth.

TEACHING MATERIAL.
Parts and functions. Root anchors the plant and absorbs water/minerals from soil. Stem holds the plant up and carries water and food between roots and leaves. Leaf makes food using sunlight (photosynthesis — made formal later) and lets the plant "breathe." Flower is the plant's reproductive part; it becomes fruit. Fruit protects and carries seeds, which grow into new plants.
Types of plants. Trees — tall, thick woody stems (mango, neem). Shrubs — medium, bushy, woody near base (rose, hibiscus). Herbs — small, soft stems (coriander, mint). Climbers — weak stems needing support (grapevine). Creepers — spread along the ground (pumpkin).
Needs. Air, water, sunlight, soil (nutrients) and space. Remove any, and the plant weakens.
Uses. Food (grains, fruits, vegetables), oxygen, wood, medicine, shade, and holding soil together.
Growth. A seed sprouts (germination) → tiny root and shoot → leaves → mature plant → flowers → fruit/seeds → new plants (life cycle).

Table — plant types.
 Type  |  Stem  |  Height  |  Example 
------------------------------
 Tree  |  thick, woody  |  tall  |  mango 
 Shrub  |  woody, bushy  |  medium  |  rose 
 Herb  |  soft, green  |  short  |  mint 
 Climber  |  weak, needs support  |  varies  |  grapevine 
 Creeper  |  weak, along ground  |  low  |  pumpkin 
Visual/diagram description. A labelled plant: roots underground, stem rising, leaves branching, a flower turning into a fruit with seeds inside.

DEFINITIONS (10). root, stem, leaf, flower, fruit, seed, germination, herb, shrub, climber.

Q&A. Easy: Which part makes food? (Leaf.) Which anchors the plant? (Root.) Medium: Give an example of a creeper (pumpkin). What does a stem carry? (Water and food.) Application: A plant kept in a dark cupboard — what happens? (It becomes weak/yellow; no sunlight to make food.) Reasoning: Why do desert plants have deep/spread roots? (To reach and gather scarce water.)

MCQs (10, abridged). Food-making part: leaf; anchors plant: root; becomes fruit: flower; soft-stem small plant: herb; needs support: climber; seed grows in: soil; a tree example: mango; plants release: oxygen; seed sprouting is called: germination; carries water up: stem.

T/F (10). Roots make food F (leaves do). Herbs have soft stems T. Flowers become fruits T. Plants need sunlight T. A creeper climbs a wall F (spreads on ground; a climber climbs). Seeds are inside fruits T. Stems carry water T. Plants give out carbon dioxide only F (release oxygen in light). Mango is a shrub F (tree). Germination is a seed sprouting T.

FILL BLANKS (10). The  makes food (leaf). The  holds the plant upright (stem). Seeds are found inside the  (fruit). A small soft-stemmed plant is a  (herb). A plant needs air, water, soil and  (sunlight). Roots absorb  from soil (water/minerals). A  needs support to grow up (climber). Sprouting of a seed is  (germination).  is the reproductive part (flower). A rose plant is a  (shrub).

SHORT/LONG. Short: List plant parts with one function each. Long (model): "Describe how a seed becomes a fruiting plant." Model — A seed germinates when given water/warmth; a root grows down and a shoot up; leaves develop and make food; the plant matures and produces flowers; pollinated flowers become fruits holding new seeds, continuing the cycle.

HOTS. Two identical plants: one watered, one not — predict and explain the difference after a week. (Unwatered wilts/dries; water is essential for transport and turgor.) Why are green leaves important even to humans? (They release oxygen we breathe and are the base of food chains.)

MISCONCEPTIONS. "Roots make food" → leaves do. "All plants are trees" → herbs/shrubs/climbers/creepers exist. "Plants don't need air" → they do (for respiration and photosynthesis).

REVISION. Parts & functions (root/stem/leaf/flower/fruit/seed); plant types; needs (air, water, sunlight, soil); uses; germination → life cycle.

ADAPTIVE — "why leaves are green / make food". L1: "Leaves make the plant's food using sunlight." L2: "Green leaves catch sunlight and, with water and air, make food for the whole plant — that's why a plant in the dark gets weak." L3: "Chlorophyll in leaves absorbs light energy to convert CO₂ and water into glucose and oxygen (photosynthesis)." L4: "A leaf is like a tiny solar kitchen — sunlight is the stove, water and air are the ingredients, and sugar is the meal it cooks." L5: "The grain and vegetables on your plate are food that leaves made using the sun — so sunlight ends up feeding you too."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain the parts of a plant to a Grade 3 student."},{"role":"assistant","content":"A plant has jobs for each part! ROOTS grip the soil and drink water. The STEM stands tall and carries water and food up and down. LEAVES are little kitchens that make food using sunlight. FLOWERS are the pretty part that turns into FRUIT, and inside the fruit are SEEDS that grow into new plants. Can you point to these parts on a plant near you?"}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Why does a plant die without sunlight? Simple please."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Because leaves need sunlight to cook the plant's food! Without light, the leaves can't make food, so the plant slowly gets weak, turns pale yellow, and can die — just like we'd get weak with no meals. Sunlight, water, air and soil together keep a plant healthy."}]}

SUBJECT: ENGLISH — Santoor (Class 3) & Hindi — Veena (compact)
RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 3  |  NCERT  |  English (Santoor)  |  CONCEPT: Grammar & writing
EXPLANATION: Nouns (common/proper, singular/plural), pronouns (he/she/it/they), verbs & simple present tense, adjectives, articles a/an/the, punctuation (. ? !); paragraph & short story reading with wh-comprehension; guided writing (5–6 sentences), simple letters/messages.  |  QUESTION: Pronoun for "the girls"? ANSWER: they.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 3  |  NCERT  |  Hindi (Veena)  |  CONCEPT: व्याकरण व पठन
EXPLANATION: वर्णमाला, मात्राएँ, संज्ञा, सर्वनाम, वचन, लिंग; सरल पठन व लेखन; अनुच्छेद व कहानी आकलन।  |  QUESTION: 'लड़का' का बहुवचन? ANSWER: लड़के।  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

===== GRADE 4 (NCERT) =====
Class 4 continues Maths Mela, Santoor, Veena, Our Wondrous World. Concepts deepen: multi-digit multiplication/division, factors/multiples, fractions, geometry, perimeter/area intro, larger measurement, data.

CHAPTER G4M-A — Fractions (Introduction) — FULL APPARATUS (anchor)
METADATA. Grade 4 · NCERT · Maths · Prereq: division/sharing (G3) · Medium · Objectives: understand a fraction as equal parts of a whole/collection; read/write fractions; identify numerator/denominator; compare simple fractions; find equivalent fractions; recognise halves/thirds/quarters; add/subtract like fractions.

TEACHING MATERIAL.
What is a fraction? When a whole is divided into equal parts, a fraction names some of those parts. In ¾: the denominator (4) tells how many equal parts the whole is cut into; the numerator (3) tells how many are taken. Parts must be equal — three unequal pieces are not thirds.
Fractions of a collection. Half of 8 apples = 4; a quarter of 12 = 3. "Fraction of a set" links to division.
Equivalent fractions. Different fractions can name the same amount: ½ = 2/4 = 3/6. Multiplying numerator and denominator by the same number keeps the value.
Comparing. With the same denominator, the bigger numerator is bigger (3/5 > 2/5). With the same numerator, the smaller denominator is bigger (½ > ⅓, because halves are larger pieces than thirds).
Add/subtract like fractions. Same denominator: add/subtract numerators, keep the denominator (2/7 + 3/7 = 5/7).
Types. Proper fraction (numerator < denominator, e.g., ¾), whole = 4/4 = 1.

Worked examples.
1. Shade 3 of 4 equal parts → ¾. 2. ½ of 10 = 5. 3. Is ½ = 3/6? Yes (×3 top and bottom). 4. 4/9 + 2/9 = 6/9.

Visual/diagram description. A circle cut into 4 equal parts with 3 shaded = ¾; beside it a bar split into 2 (½) aligned with a bar split into 4 showing 2/4 covering the same length.

DEFINITIONS (10). fraction, whole, equal parts, numerator, denominator, proper fraction, unit fraction, equivalent fractions, like fractions, half/third/quarter.

Q&A. Easy: Write "three out of five" as a fraction (3/5). What is the denominator in ⅜? (8.) Medium: Which is bigger, ½ or ⅓? (½.) ½ of 14? (7.) Difficult: Find a fraction equal to ⅔ with denominator 9 (6/9). 5/8 − 2/8? (3/8.) Application: A pizza is cut into 8 slices; you eat 3 — what fraction is left? (5/8.) Reasoning: Why is ⅕ smaller than ¼? (Cutting into more parts makes each part smaller.)

MCQs (12, abridged). ¾ numerator: 3; denominator of 2/7: 7; ½ = 2/4; bigger: ½ or ⅓ → ½; ⅓ of 9: 3; 1/5+2/5: 3/5; whole as fraction of fourths: 4/4; equal parts needed for thirds: 3; ½ of 20: 10; 6/8−2/8: 4/8; proper fraction: 3/5; ¼ of 12: 3.

T/F (10). In ¾ the 4 is the numerator F (denominator). ½ = 3/6 T. Parts of a fraction must be equal T. ⅓ > ½ F. 2/5 + 1/5 = 3/5 T. 4/4 = 1 T. ⅕ is bigger than ¼ F. Numerator counts the parts taken T. Like fractions have the same denominator T. ½ of 8 is 5 F (4).

FILL BLANKS (10). In 5/8, numerator is  (5). ½ = /4 (2). ⅓ of 12 =  (4). 2/9 + 4/9 = /9 (6). ¼ of 16 =  (4). Bigger of ¾ and ¼ is  (¾). 3/6 = /2 (1). Whole = /5 in fifths (5). Denominator of ⅐ is  (7). ½ of 18 =  (9).

SHORT/LONG. Short: Compare 3/7 and 5/7; explain. Long (model): "Explain equivalent fractions with ½." Model — ½ means 1 of 2 equal parts. If each part is split again into 2, we get 2 of 4 parts (2/4) covering the same amount; split into 3 → 3/6. Multiplying top and bottom by the same number gives equivalent fractions: ½ = 2/4 = 3/6, all equal in value.

HOTS. Two children share 3 chocolates equally — how much each? (1½ or 3/2.) Which is more: ⅔ or ¾? Justify. (¾; with equal wholes, 3/4 covers more — compare via 8/12 vs 9/12.) Order ½, ⅓, ¼ largest first (½, ⅓, ¼).

MISCONCEPTIONS. "Bigger denominator = bigger fraction" → false; more parts means smaller each. "Any parts make a fraction" → parts must be equal. "Numerator and denominator can be added straight across when adding fractions" → only numerators add for like fractions; denominators stay.

REVISION. Fraction = equal parts; numerator/denominator; equivalent fractions (×same); compare (same denom → numerator; same numerator → denom); add/subtract like fractions.
Fact sheet: ½=2/4=3/6…; a/b + c/b = (a+c)/b; whole = n/n = 1.

ADAPTIVE — "fraction". L1: "A fraction is a part of a whole that's cut into equal pieces." L2: "¾ means the whole is cut into 4 equal parts and we take 3 of them — like 3 of 4 equal pizza slices." L3: "A fraction a/b (b≠0) denotes a÷b — a parts of a whole partitioned into b equal parts; equivalence classes give the rationals." L4: "A fraction is a fair-sharing recipe: the bottom number is how many friends share, the top is how many shares you're talking about." L5: "If a chocolate bar has 8 squares and you eat 3, you ate 3/8 of it — fractions describe everyday sharing and leftovers."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain fractions to a Grade 4 kid simply."},{"role":"assistant","content":"A fraction is a piece of a whole — but the pieces must be EQUAL! Cut a roti into 4 equal parts and take 3: that's 3/4. The bottom number (4) tells how many equal parts in all; the top number (3) tells how many you took. Try shading 2 of 5 equal boxes — what fraction is that? (2/5!)"}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Why is 1/3 bigger than 1/4?"},{"role":"assistant","content":"Because the more pieces you cut a whole into, the SMALLER each piece becomes. Cut a cake into 3 — big slices (1/3). Cut it into 4 — smaller slices (1/4). Same cake, more cuts, tinier pieces. So 1/3 > 1/4. A bigger bottom number means a smaller fraction!"}]}

===== GRADE 5 (NCERT) =====
Class 5 (Maths Mela, Santoor, Veena, Our Wondrous World). Concepts: large numbers & operations, factors/multiples with HCF/LCM readiness, fractions & decimals, geometry (angles), area/perimeter/volume, data & average, and EVS moving toward science (body systems, natural phenomena, maps).

CHAPTER G5M-A — Decimals (Introduction) — FULL APPARATUS (anchor)
METADATA. Grade 5 · NCERT · Maths · Prereq: fractions, place value · Medium · Objectives: understand decimals as tenths/hundredths; read/write decimals; place value after the point; relate fractions↔decimals; compare/order; add/subtract decimals; use in money and measurement.

TEACHING MATERIAL.
Why decimals? Some quantities fall between whole numbers — ₹2 and 50 paise, or 1 metre 5 cm. Decimals extend place value to the right of a decimal point for parts smaller than one.
Place value after the point. First place = tenths (1/10 = 0.1), second = hundredths (1/100 = 0.01). So 0.7 = 7/10; 0.05 = 5/100; 0.35 = 35/100 = 3 tenths + 5 hundredths.
Fraction ↔ decimal. ½ = 5/10 = 0.5; ¼ = 25/100 = 0.25; 3/10 = 0.3.
Reading. 2.35 = "two point three five" = 2 wholes + 3 tenths + 5 hundredths.
Comparing. Line up decimal points; compare place by place from the left. 0.7 > 0.65 (7 tenths > 6 tenths). Adding zeros to the right doesn't change value (0.7 = 0.70).
Add/subtract. Line up the decimal points (and places) and add/subtract as usual, keeping the point aligned. 2.35 + 1.4 = 2.35 + 1.40 = 3.75.
Money & measure. ₹2.50 = 2 rupees 50 paise; 1.05 m = 1 m 5 cm.

Worked examples.
1. Write 7/10 and 9/100 as decimals → 0.7 and 0.09. 2. 0.6 + 0.25 = 0.85. 3. 3.5 − 1.75 = 1.75. 4. Compare 0.4 and 0.40 → equal.

Visual/diagram description. A 10×10 grid = 1 whole; shading 35 squares shows 0.35 = 35/100; a single column shaded = 0.1 (a tenth).

DEFINITIONS (10). decimal, decimal point, tenths, hundredths, decimal place, whole part, fractional part, equivalent decimal, place-value alignment, terminating decimal.

Q&A. Easy: Write 3/10 as a decimal (0.3). Read 0.9 (nine tenths). Medium: 0.5 + 0.3 (0.8). Which is bigger, 0.7 or 0.07? (0.7.) Difficult: 4.2 − 1.65 (2.55). Convert ¼ to a decimal (0.25). Application: A pen costs ₹12.50 and a book ₹35.75 — total? (₹48.25.) Reasoning: Why is 0.5 = 0.50? (Adding a zero in the hundredths place adds nothing; both are five tenths.)

MCQs (12, abridged). 0.1 = 1/10; 7/10 = 0.7; tenths digit of 3.46: 4; 0.6+0.2: 0.8; bigger: 0.9 or 0.19 → 0.9; ¼ = 0.25; 0.05 = 5/100; 2.5−1.5: 1.0; ₹0.50 = 50 paise; 0.3+0.07: 0.37; equal to 0.4: 0.40; hundredths in 0.28: 8.

T/F (10). 0.5 = 5/10 T. 0.7 < 0.07 F. 0.4 = 0.40 T. The first place after the point is hundredths F (tenths). ¼ = 0.25 T. To add decimals, line up the points T. 1.2 + 0.8 = 2.0 T. 0.9 has 9 hundredths F (9 tenths). Money uses two decimal places for paise T. 3/10 = 0.03 F (0.3).

FILL BLANKS (10). 9/10 =  (0.9). The 2nd place after the point is  (hundredths). 0.5 + 0.25 =  (0.75). ½ as a decimal =  (0.5). 0.6 = /10 (6). ₹3.75 = 3 rupees  paise (75). 0.40 =  (0.4). 2.3 + 1.4 =  (3.7). Tenths digit of 5.82 is  (8). 25/100 =  (0.25).

SHORT/LONG. Short: Add 12.6 + 3.45 (16.05). Long (model): "Explain how decimals extend place value and convert ¾ to a decimal." Model — Place value continues to the right of the point: tenths, hundredths, etc. ¾ = 75/100 (multiply top and bottom of ¾ by 25), and 75/100 = 0.75. So ¾ = 0.75, i.e., 7 tenths + 5 hundredths.

HOTS. Arrange 0.5, 0.45, 0.505 in order (0.45, 0.5, 0.505). Why does money always show two decimal places (₹5.00)? (Paise are hundredths of a rupee.) Find a decimal between 0.3 and 0.4 (e.g., 0.35).

MISCONCEPTIONS. "More digits after the point = bigger" → false (0.19 < 0.7). "0.5 and 0.05 are the same" → tenths vs hundredths. "Ignore the point when adding" → align points first.

REVISION. Decimal = place value past the point (tenths, hundredths); fraction↔decimal; compare by aligning points; add/subtract with aligned points; money/measure uses.
Fact sheet: 0.1=1/10, 0.01=1/100; ½=0.5, ¼=0.25, ¾=0.75; trailing zeros don't change value.

ADAPTIVE — "decimal". L1: "A decimal shows parts of a whole using a dot, like 0.5 for a half." L2: "After the decimal point, the first place is tenths and the next is hundredths — so 0.35 is 3 tenths and 5 hundredths, which is 35/100." L3: "Decimals extend base-10 positional notation to negative powers of ten (10⁻¹, 10⁻²…), representing fractions with denominators that are powers of ten." L4: "A decimal is a ruler zoomed in between the whole-number marks — the point separates whole metres from the smaller centimetre bits." L5: "Prices like ₹49.99 are decimals — the part after the point is paise (hundredths of a rupee)."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.

BRANCH B — MAHARASHTRA STATE BOARD (Balbharati)

Std 3–5 subjects: Marathi, Hindi, English, गणित (Mathematics), and EVS split into परिसर अभ्यास भाग १ (science-leaning) and भाग २ (social-leaning). This EVS split is a defining Maharashtra feature and is preserved (do not merge with the single NCERT EVS book).

===== GRADE 3–5 (Maharashtra) — Anchors + Compact =====

MATHEMATICS (गणित) — Anchor: Multiplication & पाढे (Std 3) → Fractions/Decimals (Std 4–5)
METADATA. Grade 3–5 · Maharashtra · Mathematics · Medium · Objectives mirror NCERT concepts (multiplication, division, fractions, decimals, geometry, measurement, data) but follow the Balbharati sequence with Marathi terminology and its well-known emphasis on पाढे (times-tables) mastery.

TEACHING (key points, original). गुणाकार (multiplication) as सारखे गट जोडणे and arrays; पाढे 2–10 (and up to 20 in higher stds) practised for fluency; भागाकार (division) as समान वाटप/गटीकरण with बाकी (remainder); अपूर्णांक (fractions) as समान भागांतील भाग (अंश numerator/छेद denominator); दशांश (decimals) as दशांश चिन्हानंतरचे भाग; भूमिती (geometry) व मापन (measurement) in metric units; सामग्री/माहिती (data) as चित्रालेख/स्तंभालेख.

Difference to preserve (NCERT vs Maharashtra): Maharashtra pushes rote पाढे and larger number-ranges earlier and keeps a distinct Marathi metalanguage (अंश/छेद, बाकी, दशांश), while NCERT Maths Mela front-loads conceptual/array-based understanding and themed activity chapters. Same mathematics, different sequence and language — keep branches separate.

ADAPTIVE — "अपूर्णांक (fraction)". L1: "अपूर्णांक म्हणजे पूर्ण गोष्टीचा समान भाग." L2: "एक पोळी ४ समान भागांत कापून ३ घेतले तर ३/४ — छेद (४) एकूण भाग, अंश (३) घेतलेले भाग." L3: "a/b (b≠0) म्हणजे a ÷ b; समान भागांची संख्या छेदाने वाढल्यास प्रत्येक भाग लहान होतो." L4: "मित्रांमध्ये वाटपाची कृती — खालचा आकडा किती जण वाटून घेतात, वरचा किती भाग." L5: "चॉकलेटच्या ८ तुकड्यांतले ३ खाल्ले = ३/८ खाल्ले."

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 3  |  NCERT  |  Maths  |  UNIT: Operations  |  CHAPTER: Give and Take  |  SECTION: Multiplication  |  CONCEPT: Repeated addition/arrays
PREREQUISITES: Skip counting, addition  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Multiply & use tables/commutativity
EXPLANATION: a×b = adding b, a times = an a-by-b array; a×b=b×a; ×0=0, ×1=same, ×10 appends 0.  |  EXAMPLE: 4×6=24  |  APPLICATION: Rows of chairs, packs  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: ×0 gives the number  |  QUESTION: 27×6  |  ANSWER: 162  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 3  |  NCERT  |  Maths  |  CHAPTER: Fair Share  |  SECTION: Division  |  CONCEPT: Equal sharing/grouping, remainder
PREREQUISITES: Multiplication  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Divide within tables with remainder
EXPLANATION: Division shares/groups equally; inverse of ×; remainder < divisor; n÷0 undefined.  |  EXAMPLE: 13÷4=3 r1  |  APPLICATION: Sharing sweets, seating rows  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Division is commutative  |  QUESTION: 22÷5  |  ANSWER: 4 r 2  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 4  |  NCERT  |  Maths  |  CHAPTER: Fractions  |  SECTION: Meaning  |  CONCEPT: Equal parts / numerator-denominator
PREREQUISITES: Division/sharing  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Read/compare/equivalent fractions
EXPLANATION: a/b = a of b equal parts; more parts → smaller each; ½=2/4; like fractions add numerators.  |  EXAMPLE: ¾ shaded  |  APPLICATION: Pizza slices, sharing  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Bigger denominator = bigger fraction  |  QUESTION: Compare ½ and ⅓  |  ANSWER: ½ is bigger  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 5  |  NCERT  |  Maths  |  CHAPTER: Decimals  |  SECTION: Place value  |  CONCEPT: Tenths/hundredths
PREREQUISITES: Fractions, place value  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Read/compare/add decimals
EXPLANATION: Places right of the point: tenths, hundredths; ½=0.5, ¼=0.25; align points to compute; trailing zeros keep value.  |  EXAMPLE: 0.35=35/100  |  APPLICATION: Money, measurement  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: More digits after point = bigger  |  QUESTION: Bigger: 0.7 or 0.65?  |  ANSWER: 0.7  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 3  |  NCERT  |  EVS  |  CHAPTER: Getting to Know Plants  |  SECTION: Parts & needs  |  CONCEPT: Plant structure/function
PREREQUISITES: Grade 1–2 plant awareness  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Parts, types, needs, life cycle
EXPLANATION: Root/stem/leaf/flower/fruit/seed with functions; herb/shrub/tree/climber/creeper; needs air/water/sun/soil; germination cycle.  |  EXAMPLE: Leaf makes food; flower→fruit  |  APPLICATION: Kitchen-garden observation  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Roots make food  |  QUESTION: Which part makes food?  |  ANSWER: The leaf  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 3–5  |  Maharashtra  |  EVS Part 1 & 2  |  CONCEPT: Split EVS (science vs social)
EXPLANATION: Maharashtra separates परिसर अभ्यास into भाग १ (living world, body, resources, simple science) and भाग २ (family/society, maps/directions, state & nation, civic sense) — distinct from the single integrated NCERT EVS book.  |  QUESTION: Sun rises in which direction?  |  ANSWER: East.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

===== GRADE 1 (NCERT) =====

SUBJECT: MATHEMATICS — Joyful Mathematics (Class 1)
Verified chapter arc: (1) Finding the Furry Cat! — pre-number concepts; (2) What is Long? What is Round? — shapes; (3) Mango Treat — numbers 1–9; (4) Making 10 — numbers 10–20; (5) How Many? — counting & grouping; (6) addition; (7) subtraction; (8) larger numbers to ~99; (9) measurement; (10) patterns; (11) time; (12) money; (13) So Many Toys — data handling.


CHAPTER G1M-1 — Pre-number Concepts ("Finding the Furry Cat!")

METADATA. Grade 1 · Board NCERT/CBSE · Subject Mathematics · Unit Number readiness · Prerequisites: KG counting to 10 · Difficulty: Easy · Objectives: use position words (in/out, above/below, near/far); compare (big/small, more/less, heavy/light); sort and classify objects; recognise "as many as" (one-to-one matching).

TEACHING MATERIAL.
Before numbers come ideas about quantity and position. A child must be able to say where a thing is and whether one group has more than another before formal counting means anything.
• Position/spatial words. Inside/outside (the cat is inside the basket), above/below, on/under, near/far, front/behind, left/right. These describe where things are.
• Comparison words. Big/small, tall/short, long/short, heavy/light, more/less/equal, fast/slow. Comparison always needs two things: "The elephant is bigger than the cat."
• Sorting/classifying. Grouping things that are alike — by colour, shape, size, or kind. Sorting trains the logic that underlies place value later.
• One-to-one matching ("as many as"). Draw a line from each cup to one saucer. If none is left over on either side, there are "as many cups as saucers" — equal. This is the seed of the equals sign.

Visual/diagram description. A basket with three kittens inside and two outside; arrows label inside/outside. Below, cups matched to saucers by connecting lines.

Common misconceptions. "The taller glass has more water" — not always; a wide short glass can hold more. Compare by matching or measuring, not by looking. "More things = takes more space" — five marbles take less space than one football.

DEFINITIONS (10). 1. Compare — to see how two things differ in size/number. 2. Sort — group alike things. 3. Classify — sort by a chosen rule. 4. More — greater amount. 5. Less — smaller amount. 6. Equal / as many as — same number. 7. Inside — within the boundary. 8. Outside — beyond the boundary. 9. Heavy — hard to lift; more weight. 10. One-to-one matching — pairing each item of one group with one of another.

Q&A. Easy: Is a bus big or small next to a cycle? (Big.) Where is a fish — inside or outside water? (Inside.) Medium: If 4 spoons match 4 bowls with none left, are they equal? (Yes.) Sort: apple, ball, banana — by "fruit." (Apple, banana.) Application: You have 3 red and 5 blue beads — which is more? (Blue.) Put the toy under the table — where is it? (Below the table.)

MCQs (10, abridged). 1. Opposite of big — (A)small (B)tall (C)more (D)up. 2. The cat sits  the box (it's covered): (A)inside (B)far (C)fast (D)equal. 3. Which is heavy? (A)feather (B)stone (C)paper (D)leaf. 4. 3 cups, 3 plates matched, none left → (A)more cups (B)more plates (C)equal (D)none. 5. Which holds more? (A)spoon (B)bucket (C)cup (D)glass. 6. Opposite of fast — (A)slow (B)big (C)near (D)more. 7. Sort out the odd one: dog, cat, car, cow → car (not an animal). 8. Fewer: 2 or 6? (A)2 (B)6. 9. Near is opposite of — (A)far (B)small (C)under. 10. A group of only round things should include — (A)ball (B)book (C)box (D)brick.

T/F (5). More means a smaller amount — F (greater). A stone is heavier than a feather — T. Inside and outside mean the same — F. Matching each cup to a saucer checks equality — T. A short wide glass can hold more than a tall thin one — T.

FILL BLANKS (5). Opposite of tall is  (short). If none is left over after matching, the groups are  (equal). A rock is  than a leaf (heavier). The cat is  the basket when covered (inside). 6 is  than 2 (more).

HOTS. Two glasses look different but hold the same water — how can you check? (Pour one into the other.) You matched forks to plates and 1 fork is left over — which is more? (Forks, by one.)

MISCONCEPTIONS corrected. Taller ≠ more (depends on width). More objects ≠ more space. Comparison needs two items, not one.

REVISION. Position words, comparison words (need two things), sorting by a rule, one-to-one matching → equal/more/less.

ADAPTIVE — "as many as / equal". L1: "Equal means the same number." L2: "Match each cup to a saucer; if none is left, there are as many cups as saucers — equal." L3: "Equality of two sets means a bijection exists — every element pairs with exactly one in the other set." L4: "Like musical chairs where every child gets a chair and no chair is empty — a perfect match." L5: "One roti per person at dinner: if plates and rotis run out together, they were equal."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.
``json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"How do I explain 'more and less' to a Grade 1 kid?"},{"role":"assistant","content":"Use matching, not guessing! Line up the two groups and pair one-to-one: put each red bead next to a blue bead. Whichever group has leftovers has MORE; the other has LESS; if none is left over, they're EQUAL. This works even before the child can count the exact numbers."}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Give me a tricky question on comparing sizes."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Here's one: A tall thin glass and a short wide glass — which holds more water? Trick answer: you can't tell just by looking at the height! Pour one glass into the other to find out. Height alone doesn't decide 'more'."}]}

CHAPTER G1M-2 — Shapes ("What is Long? What is Round?")
METADATA. Grade 1 · NCERT · Maths · Unit Geometry basics · Prereq G1M-1 · Easy · Objectives: identify 2-D shapes (circle, triangle, square, rectangle) and 3-D solids (ball/sphere, box/cuboid, can/cylinder); describe by rolls/slides, corners, sides; find shapes in surroundings.

TEACHING MATERIAL. Flat (2-D) shapes have length and width but no thickness — circle, triangle, square, rectangle. Solid (3-D) shapes take up space — a ball (sphere) rolls, a box (cuboid) slides and stacks, a can (cylinder) rolls on its side and stacks on its ends, a dice (cube) has 6 equal faces. We describe solids by whether they roll or slide, how many corners, faces, and edges. Circle: curved, no corner. Triangle: 3 sides, 3 corners. Square: 4 equal sides. Rectangle: 4 sides, opposite sides equal.

Table — solids.
 Solid  |  Rolls?  |  Slides/Stacks?  |  Real example 
------------------------------
 Sphere (ball)  |  Yes  |  No  |  football 
 Cube (dice)  |  No  |  Yes  |  dice, box 
 Cuboid (brick)  |  No  |  Yes  |  matchbox 
 Cylinder (can)  |  Yes (on side)  |  Stacks on ends  |  tin, glass 
 Cone  |  Rolls in circle  |  Stacks point-up  |  ice-cream cone 
Common misconceptions. A ball is not a "circle" — a circle is flat, a ball is a solid (sphere). A square is a special rectangle (all sides equal), but at Grade 1 we treat them as separate named shapes.

DEFINITIONS (10). flat shape, solid shape, circle, triangle, square, rectangle, corner (vertex), side, roll, slide.

Q&A. Easy: How many corners has a triangle? (3.) Does a ball roll? (Yes.) Medium: Name a shape with 4 equal sides (square). Which solid can both roll and stack? (Cylinder.) Application: Find a rectangle in the room (door/book). Which solid is a dice? (Cube.)

MCQs (10, abridged). Ball is a — sphere; dice has 6 faces; triangle has 3 corners; a can is a cylinder; circle has 0 corners; door ≈ rectangle; which rolls: ball; square has 4 equal sides; ice-cream cone shape: cone; stack biscuits like a — cylinder (tin).

T/F (5). A ball is a flat shape — F (solid). A cube has 6 faces — T. A circle has 3 corners — F (none). A cylinder can roll — T. A rectangle has 4 sides — T.

FILL BLANKS (5). A triangle has  sides (3). A  rolls (ball/sphere). A dice has  faces (6). A circle has  corners (0). A door is like a  (rectangle).

HOTS. Why can you stack cans but a ball rolls away? (Flat ends vs. curved surface.) Sort solids into "rolls" and "does not roll." (Rolls: ball, cylinder, cone; not: cube, cuboid.)

REVISION. 2-D flat vs 3-D solid; corners/sides/faces; roll vs slide; real-world shapes.

ADAPTIVE — "sphere vs circle". L1: "A circle is flat; a ball is round like an orange." L2: "A circle is drawn on paper (2-D); a sphere is a solid you can hold (3-D)." L3: "A circle is a 2-D locus of points equidistant from a centre; a sphere is its 3-D analogue." L4: "A circle is the shadow a ball casts on the ground — flat — while the ball itself is full and solid." L5: "A coin's face is a circle; the marble is a sphere."

INSTRUCTION RECORD.
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain why some shapes roll for a first grader."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Shapes with a CURVED surface roll — like a ball or a can lying on its side, because there are no flat corners to stop them. Shapes with only FLAT faces — like a box or dice — slide and stack but don't roll. Try it: push a tin on its side (it rolls!) then stand it up (it stacks!)."}]}
`


CHAPTER G1M-3 — Numbers 1 to 9 ("Mango Treat")
METADATA. Grade 1 · NCERT · Maths · Prereq counting readiness · Easy · Objectives: count, read and write numerals 1–9; understand cardinality (last count = how many); order and compare; recognise zero as "none"; ordinal ideas (first–ninth).

TEACHING MATERIAL. Each number has a quantity, a number-word, and a numeral. Zero (0) means nothing/none — an empty plate has 0 mangoes. Cardinality: the last number you say when counting tells how many altogether. Ordinals: first, second, third… tell position in a line. We compare using more/less/equal and the words greater/smaller. Writing numerals: correct stroke order (e.g., 2 starts with a curve then a flat base).

Table. number-word ↔ numeral ↔ quantity for 0–9 (zero=•empty; one=•; two=••; … nine=•••••••••).

Common misconceptions. Zero is a number (means "none"), not "nothing to write." Counting the last word = total (cardinality), not just reciting. Numeral reversal (writing 3 backwards, or 6/9 confusion) — corrected by starting-point cues.

DEFINITIONS (10). numeral, number-word, zero, count, cardinal number, ordinal number, greater than, smaller than, order, digit.

Q&A. Easy: Write the numeral for seven (7). How many are in an empty box? (0.) Medium: Which is greater, 6 or 9? (9.) Who is 3rd in line if Asha is 1st? (the third child.) Application: Arrange 4, 1, 7 smallest first (1,4,7). Count 8 mangoes and say the total (8).

MCQs (10, abridged). Numeral for five: 5; "none" is written as 0; greater of 3 and 8: 8; after 6 comes 7; before 5 is 4; 2nd position word: second; smallest of 9,2,5: 2; how many dots ••• : 3; largest 1-digit number: 9; order 3,1,2 smallest-first: 1,2,3.

T/F (5). Zero means none — T. 9 is smaller than 6 — F. The last count word tells how many — T. After 8 comes 10 — F (9). "First" tells position — T.

FILL BLANKS (5). After 4 comes  (5).  means none (0/zero). The greatest 1-digit number is  (9). Before 7 is  (6). 3rd position is called  (third).

HOTS. If you eat all 5 mangoes, how many are left and what number is that? (0.) Riya is 2nd, Sam is just behind her — what position is Sam? (3rd.)

REVISION. 0–9 quantity/word/numeral; zero=none; cardinality; ordinals; compare & order.

ADAPTIVE — "zero". L1: "Zero means nothing is there." L2: "If you had 3 sweets and ate all 3, you have 0 — zero — left." L3: "Zero is the additive identity and the cardinality of the empty set." L4: "Zero is an empty basket — it's still a basket (a number), just with nothing in it." L5: "The scoreboard shows 0 before the match starts — no goals yet."

INSTRUCTION RECORD.
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain zero to a 6-year-old."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Zero means NONE — nothing there! If you have 4 biscuits and eat all 4, how many are left? Zero! We write it like a smooth oval: 0. Zero is a real number — it's the 'empty' number that tells us there's nothing to count right now."}]}
`


CHAPTERS G1M-4 to G1M-13 — Compact LESSON records
(Full teaching apparatus available on request; these anchor the remaining Grade-1 NCERT maths arc.)

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Making 10 (Numbers 10–20)  |  CONCEPT: Teen numbers & bundling
EXPLANATION: 10 is one bundle of ten. Teen numbers are ten and some ones: 13 = 1 ten + 3 ones. Number pairs that make 10 (0+10, 1+9, 2+8, 3+7, 4+6, 5+5) are key facts.
EXAMPLE: 10 straws tied in a bundle + 4 loose = 14.  |  MISCONCEPTION: "13 means 3 then 1" — place matters; the 1 is a ten.  |  QUESTION: What makes 10 with 7? ANSWER: 3.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: How Many? (Counting & grouping)  |  CONCEPT: Grouping to count faster
EXPLANATION: Counting in groups (2s, 5s, 10s) is quicker and more accurate than by 1s for larger sets.  |  EXAMPLE: 20 pencils in 4 groups of 5.  |  QUESTION: Count 15 in fives. ANSWER: 5,10,15.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Addition  |  CONCEPT: Combining/counting on
EXPLANATION: Addition combines groups; count on from the larger number. Symbols + and =. Adding 0 changes nothing. Order can be swapped (2+5=5+2).  |  EXAMPLE: 6+3: "six… seven, eight, nine" = 9.  |  MISCONCEPTION: Recounting both groups from 1.  |  QUESTION: 7+2? ANSWER: 9.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Subtraction  |  CONCEPT: Take away / difference / count back
EXPLANATION: Subtraction removes from a group or finds the difference; sign is −. a−a=0; a−0=a. Count back for small numbers.  |  EXAMPLE: 8−3: "seven, six, five" = 5.  |  MISCONCEPTION: Subtraction is not commutative (5−2 ≠ 2−5).  |  QUESTION: 9−4? ANSWER: 5.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Numbers to 99  |  CONCEPT: Tens and ones (place value)
EXPLANATION: A two-digit number has a tens place and a ones place: 47 = 4 tens + 7 ones. Count in tens to 90; fill ones between.  |  EXAMPLE: 52 = 5 bundles of ten + 2 loose.  |  MISCONCEPTION: Reading 52 as "5 and 2" instead of "fifty-two".  |  QUESTION: What is 6 tens + 3 ones? ANSWER: 63.  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Measurement  |  CONCEPT: Non-standard length/weight/capacity
EXPLANATION: Measure length in hand-spans/steps, weight by balance (heavier side goes down), capacity by how many cups fill a jug — before standard units.  |  EXAMPLE: The table is 6 hand-spans long.  |  QUESTION: Which is heavier if the balance tips towards it? ANSWER: That object.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Patterns  |  CONCEPT: Repeating & growing patterns
EXPLANATION: A pattern follows a rule and repeats (AB, ABB, AAB) or grows (1,2,3…). Identify the "unit" that repeats, then extend.  |  EXAMPLE: ▲■▲■ → ▲.  |  QUESTION: Next: red, red, blue, red, red, ? ANSWER: blue.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Time  |  CONCEPT: Sequence & day/night, days, o'clock
EXPLANATION: Order events (morning→noon→night); name days of the week; read "o'clock" on a clock (short hand = hour).  |  EXAMPLE: We sleep at night, eat lunch at noon.  |  QUESTION: Day after Monday? ANSWER: Tuesday.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Money  |  CONCEPT: Coins/notes & simple value
EXPLANATION: Recognise ₹1,₹2,₹5,₹10 coins and ₹10/₹20 notes; combine small coins to a value; more money buys more.  |  EXAMPLE: ₹5 = five ₹1 coins.  |  QUESTION: Two ₹2 coins = ? ANSWER: ₹4.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE: 1  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: So Many Toys (Data handling)  |  CONCEPT: Sorting & simple pictographs
EXPLANATION: Collect, sort, and show data as tally marks or pictures (one picture = one item); read "which has most/least".  |  EXAMPLE: 3 cars, 5 balls → balls are most.  |  QUESTION: If red=4, blue=6, which is most? ANSWER: Blue.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy


SUBJECT: ENGLISH — Mridang (Class 1)
Nine units, each pairing a poem and a story with picture-talk and activities. Copyright note: poems/stories are NOT reproduced; below are the skills and themes they build, plus original teaching content.

CHAPTER G1E-1 — Early Literacy Skills (anchor)
METADATA. Grade 1 · NCERT · English · Unit 1–2 skills · Prereq KG phonics · Easy · Objectives: revise letter sounds; blend/segment CVC words; read simple sentences; recognise naming words (nouns) and a/an; listen to a story and answer who/what/where; speak in short sentences; copy letters/words neatly.

TEACHING MATERIAL.
• Phonics & decoding. Sound out CVC and simple CVCC/CCVC words (cat, ship, frog). Segment to spell (dog → /d/ /o/ /g/).
• Sight words. the, a, is, in, it, and, to, I, you, we — read by memory.
• Naming words (nouns). Words for people, animals, places, things (boy, cow, park, ball). Names of people/places start with a capital letter.
• a / an. Use a before a consonant sound (a cat), an before a vowel sound (an apple).
• Sentence sense. A sentence starts with a capital and ends with a full stop; it tells a complete idea.
• Listening/speaking. Story-time builds vocabulary and sequence; picture-talk builds speaking.
• Handwriting. Correct formation on four lines; spacing between words.

Table — a/an. a book, a dog, a table  |  an apple, an egg, an umbrella.

Common misconceptions. "an" is used before vowel sounds, not just vowel letters (an hour). All words don't start with capitals — only sentence-starts and names.

DEFINITIONS (10). letter, sound, word, sentence, noun/naming word, vowel, consonant, capital letter, full stop, sight word.

Q&A. Easy: Read: "The cat is fat." Who is fat? (The cat.) Medium: a or an before "egg"? (an.) Name three animals (nouns). Application: Make a sentence with "dog." Point to a naming word in the room.

MCQs (10, abridged). a/an before apple: an; naming word: ball (not run); sentence ends with: full stop; capital needed for a person's name; blend d-o-g: dog; sight word: the; a  dog: a; vowel among b,e,t: e; start a sentence with a  letter: capital; "an" before: egg.

T/F (5). Names begin with a capital — T. Use "an" before "cat" — F (a cat). A sentence needs a full stop — T. "Ball" is a naming word — T. Sight words are sounded out letter by letter — F.

FILL BLANKS (5).  apple (an). A sentence ends with a  (full stop).  dog (a). Names start with a  letter (capital). c-a-t =  (cat).

HOTS. Why "an hour" but "a house" when both start with h? (Sound: hour starts with a vowel sound, house with /h/.) Turn "cat" into a naming-word sentence. (e.g., "The cat ran.")

REVISION. decode CVC, sight words, nouns, a/an by sound, sentence = capital + full stop.

ADAPTIVE — "noun". L1: "A naming word names a person, animal, place or thing." L2: "boy, cat, park, ball are nouns — they name something." L3: "A noun is a word denoting an entity — concrete or abstract — that can head a noun phrase." L4: "Nouns are the name-tags of language — every person, place and thing wears one." L5: "In 'The girl kicked the ball in the park,' the name-tags are girl, ball, park."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"When do we use a and an? Explain for a Grade 1 child."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Listen to the FIRST SOUND of the next word! If it sounds like a vowel (a, e, i, o, u sound) — say 'an': an apple, an egg, an umbrella. If it sounds like a consonant — say 'a': a cat, a book, a dog. Trick: it's about the SOUND, so we say 'an hour' (the h is silent) but 'a house'."}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Test me on naming words."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Find the naming word (the person/animal/place/thing) in each: 1) The dog barks. 2) Riya runs fast. 3) We play in the park. (Answers: dog; Riya; park.) Want a few harder ones with two naming words each?"}]}
`

===== GRADE 2 (NCERT) =====

SUBJECT: MATHEMATICS — Joyful Mathematics (Class 2)
Arc builds on Class 1: numbers to 100+ with place value; addition/subtraction with regrouping (carry/borrow) introduced; repeated addition → readiness for multiplication; equal sharing → readiness for division; measurement with standard units begins (cm, litre, gram, rupee); shapes; patterns; time (half/quarter); money; simple data.

CHAPTER G2M-1 — Place Value & Numbers to 100+ (anchor)
METADATA. Grade 2 · NCERT · Maths · Prereq G1 numbers to 99 · Medium · Objectives: read/write/order numbers to 100 (and beyond to 999 informally); expand into tens & ones; compare using < > =; skip-count by 2s/5s/10s; identify even/odd.

TEACHING MATERIAL.
• Place value. In a two-digit number the left digit is tens, the right is ones: 47 = 40 + 7 = 4 tens + 7 ones. Expanded form: 63 = 60 + 3.
• Comparing/ordering. Compare tens first, then ones. Symbols: > greater, < smaller, = equal (the open mouth "eats" the bigger number).
• Skip counting. By 2s (2,4,6…), 5s (5,10,15…), 10s (10,20,30…) — foundation for multiplication.
• Even/odd. Even numbers pair up with none left (2,4,6,8,0 at the end); odd have one left over (1,3,5,7,9 at the end).

Table — place value of 58.  |  Tens  |  Ones  |  =  |  5  |  8  |  → 50 + 8.

Common misconceptions. 45 vs 54 — same digits, different value (place matters). "Bigger digit means bigger number" — compare place by place from the left. The symbol < / > direction confuses many; teach "open side faces the bigger number."

DEFINITIONS (10). place value, tens, ones, expanded form, compare, greater than (>), smaller than (<), skip count, even, odd.

Q&A. Easy: What is the tens digit in 72? (7.) Even or odd: 8? (Even.) Medium: Expand 56 (50+6). Which is greater, 34 or 43? (43.) Application: Skip-count by 5 to 25 (5,10,15,20,25). Put >,<,= : 27  72 (<).

MCQs (10, abridged). Tens digit of 39: 3; 45 expanded: 40+5; 61  16: >; even number: 10; skip by 2 after 6: 8; smallest of 23,32,22: 22; 7 tens 0 ones = 70; odd number: 15; number after 99: 100; compare 50  50: =.

T/F (5). 45 and 54 are equal — F. 8 is even — T. The left digit of a 2-digit number is tens — T. > means smaller — F (greater). 10,20,30 is skip-counting by tens — T.

FILL BLANKS (5). 63 =  + 3 (60). The ones digit of 47 is  (7). 5,10,15 counts by  (5). 9 is an  number (odd). 27  72 (< ).

HOTS. Make the biggest 2-digit number from 3 and 7 (73) and the smallest (37). Why is 20 even but 25 odd? (20 pairs fully; 25 leaves one over.)

REVISION. tens & ones, expanded form, compare place-by-place, skip counting, even/odd.

ADAPTIVE — "place value". L1: "Where a digit sits changes how much it's worth." L2: "In 47, the 4 means 4 tens (40) and the 7 means 7 ones — position gives value." L3: "Positional notation weights each digit by a power of the base (base-10: …,10¹,10⁰)." L4: "Like seats in a stadium — the same person is worth more in the 'tens' seat than the 'ones' seat." L5: "₹40 and ₹4 use the same '4', but the place tells you it's ten times more money."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.
`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"My child mixes up 45 and 54. How to explain?"},{"role":"assistant","content":"Use bundles! Give 45 as 4 bundles of ten + 5 loose sticks = 45. Then 54 as 5 bundles + 4 loose = 54. Same digits, but the PLACE decides the value: the tens place is worth ten times the ones place. Line them up and count the bundles — the child will SEE 54 is bigger."}]}
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Give me an even/odd puzzle."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Try this: 7 children want to pair up for a game. Can everyone find a partner? (No — one child is left over, so 7 is ODD.) Now try 8 children. (Yes — all paired, so 8 is EVEN.) Even numbers pair up perfectly; odd numbers leave one out."}]}
`

CHAPTER G2M-2 — Addition & Subtraction with Regrouping (anchor, condensed)
METADATA. Grade 2 · NCERT · Maths · Prereq place value · Medium · Objectives: add/subtract 2-digit numbers with and without regrouping; use place-value columns; solve one-step word problems; check by reversing.

TEACHING MATERIAL. Add ones first, then tens. Carrying (regrouping in addition): if the ones add to 10 or more, carry one ten to the tens column (e.g., 27 + 15: 7+5=12 → write 2, carry 1; 1+2+1=4 → 42). Borrowing (regrouping in subtraction): if the top ones digit is too small, borrow one ten (make it 10 ones) (e.g., 42 − 15: 2−5 can't, borrow → 12−5=7; 3−1=2 → 27). Addition and subtraction are inverse — check 42−15=27 by 27+15=42.

Worked example. 38 + 46: ones 8+6=14 → write 4 carry 1; tens 3+4+1=8 → 84. Check: 84−46=38. ✓

Common misconceptions. Forgetting to carry; subtracting the smaller ones digit from the larger regardless of position (writing 42−15 ones as 5−2=3). Teach column alignment.

DEFINITIONS (10). sum, difference, regroup, carry, borrow, column, place-value alignment, inverse operations, one-step problem, check.

Q&A/MCQ/etc. (condensed). 27+15=42; 53−28=25; carry needed in 46+37? Yes; borrow in 71−36? Yes; check 60−24 by 36+24=60; word: "23 apples + 19 = ?" 42.

HOTS. Find the missing digit: 3 + 15 = 52 (→ 37). Why must ones line up under ones? (Same place values add together.)

ADAPTIVE — "carrying". L1: "When ones make ten or more, one ten moves to the tens column." L2: "7+5=12: keep 2 ones, carry the 1 ten next door." L3: "Regrouping conserves value across positional columns during addition overflow." L4: "Like a box that holds only 9 marbles — the 10th forces you to start a new box (a ten)." L5: "Buying items: 7 loose ₹1 coins + 5 = 12 → exchange 10 coins for one ₹10 note (a ten)."

`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Solve 38 + 46 step by step for a Grade 2 student."},{"role":"assistant","content":"Line them up, ones under ones:\n  38\n+ 46\nStep 1 (ones): 8 + 6 = 14. Write 4, carry 1 ten.\nStep 2 (tens): 3 + 4 + 1(carried) = 8. Write 8.\nAnswer: 84. Check by subtracting: 84 − 46 = 38 ✓"}]}
`

=================================================================
BRANCH B — MAHARASHTRA STATE BOARD (Balbharati)
=================================================================
Grades 1–2 use the integrated textbook (एकात्मिक पाठ्यपुस्तक) in parts (भाग 1–4), blending Marathi, English, Mathematics (गणित) and EVS/Play-Do across themed units, available in Marathi, English and Semi-English mediums. Kept separate from the NCERT branch.

===== GRADE 1 (Maharashtra) =====

SUBJECT: MARATHI (Balbharati) — Anchor
METADATA. Grade 1 · Maharashtra · Marathi (first language) · Prereq: spoken Marathi · Easy · Objectives: recognise and write मुळाक्षरे (vowels स्वर + consonants व्यंजन); read simple बारखडी (consonant+vowel matra combinations); read/write 2–3 letter words; picture-word reading; recite रांगोळी/rhymes; speak in short sentences.

TEACHING MATERIAL (original).
• स्वर (vowels): अ आ इ ई उ ऊ ए ऐ ओ औ अं अः. These are spoken with an open voice and can stand alone.
• व्यंजन (consonants): क ख ग घ … ह ळ क्ष ज्ञ. A consonant carries an inherent 'अ' sound (क = "ka").
• बारखडी (matra chart): each consonant combines with each vowel sign to form a syllable: क का कि की कु कू के कै को कौ कं कः. Reading Marathi = reading these syllables fluently.
• Words: join syllables into words — कमळ (lotus), मका (corn), घर (house). Read left to right.
• Reading/writing: correct stroke order; the top horizontal line (शिरोरेषा) joins letters.
• Listening/speaking: rhymes and stories build vocabulary and pronunciation.

Common misconceptions. A consonant is not silent — it already contains 'अ'. Matras (ा ि ी ु ू …) change the vowel sound, not the consonant. Reading is syllable-by-syllable, not letter-by-letter.

DEFINITIONS (10). मुळाक्षरे (alphabet), स्वर (vowel), व्यंजन (consonant), बारखडी (syllable chart), मात्रा/काना-मात्रा (vowel sign), अक्षर (syllable/letter), शब्द (word), वाक्य (sentence), शिरोरेषा (top line), उच्चार (pronunciation).

Q&A. Easy: पहिला स्वर कोणता? (अ.) 'क' मध्ये कोणता स्वर लपला आहे? (अ.) Medium: 'का' कसा तयार होतो? (क + आ-काना.) 'घर' मध्ये किती अक्षरे? (2: घ + र.) Application: चित्र बघून शब्द वाचा — कमळ. 'मी शाळेत जातो' — किती शब्द? (3.)

MCQ (10, abridged, English gloss). First vowel — अ; 'क' holds vowel — अ; काना (ा) makes क → का; शिरोरेषा is the top line; number of स्वर shown (अ…अः) — 12; syllables in घर — 2; 'ि' matra with क → कि; a व्यंजन example — क; joining letters uses शिरोरेषा — True; word for house — घर.

T/F (5). 'क' चा उच्चार फक्त 'क्' असा होतो — F (it's "ka", holds अ). मात्रा स्वराचा आवाज बदलते — T. मराठी डावीकडून उजवीकडे वाचतात — T. शिरोरेषा अक्षरे जोडते — T. अ हा व्यंजन आहे — F (स्वर).

FILL BLANKS (5). पहिला स्वर  (अ). क + ा =  (का). 'घर' मध्ये  अक्षरे (2). अक्षरांना जोडणारी रेषा म्हणजे  (शिरोरेषा). अ, आ, इ यांना  म्हणतात (स्वर).

HOTS. क, ख, ग यांत लपलेला समान स्वर कोणता व तो कसा ओळखायचा? (अ — प्रत्येक व्यंजनात inherent अ आहे.) 'का' आणि 'क' यांत काय फरक? (मात्रा 'ा' मुळे आ-आवाज आला.)

REVISION. 12 स्वर, व्यंजन (inherent अ), बारखडी combos, शिरोरेषा, syllable reading.

ADAPTIVE — "बारखडी / matra". L1: "एका व्यंजनाला वेगवेगळे स्वर लावले की वेगळे आवाज होतात." L2: "क ला काना लावला की का, वेलांटी लावली की कि — स्वरचिन्ह आवाज बदलते." L3: "बारखडी म्हणजे व्यंजन × स्वरचिन्ह यांचे syllabic matrix; प्रत्येक अक्षर एक अक्षर(syllable) दर्शवते." L4: "एकच व्यंजन म्हणजे रिकामी फ्रेम; मात्रा म्हणजे त्यात बसवलेला वेगळा रंग — रंग बदलला की चित्र बदलले." L5: "नावं वाचताना — कमल, किरण, कुणाल — तीच 'क' पण मात्रेमुळे वेगळा आवाज."

INSTRUCTION RECORDS.

SUBJECT: MATHEMATICS (गणित, Grade 1) — Anchor (parallels NCERT with Balbharati sequence)
METADATA. Grade 1 · Maharashtra · Mathematics · Easy · Objectives: numbers 1–100 (Maharashtra introduces up to 100 in Std 1 with number-names in Marathi/English); addition & subtraction within 20; shapes; comparison; simple patterns; Indian money & time basics.

TEACHING MATERIAL. Content parallels the NCERT arc (counting, place value seed, add/subtract, shapes, patterns, measurement, money, time) but with the Balbharati sequence and Marathi number-names (एक, दोन, तीन… शंभर). Emphasis on oral number-names in both Marathi and English for semi-English classes. Addition/subtraction taught with objects and number line to 20; two-digit reading (२१ = एकवीस = twenty-one).

Difference to preserve (NCERT vs Maharashtra): Maharashtra Std 1 commonly extends number reading toward 100 with Marathi number-names and links to daily contexts (bazaar/money in ₹), whereas NCERT Joyful Class 1 anchors more on 0–20 with playful pre-number work first. Keep both as separate branches.

DEFINITIONS/Q&A/MCQ (condensed). संख्या (number), बेरीज (addition), वजाबाकी (subtraction), आकार (shape), नमुना (pattern). Q: ५ + ३ = ? (८). Q: १० − ४ = ? (६). Q: २१ चे नाव? (एकवीस). MCQ: बेरीज चिन्ह — +; ७+२ = ९; वजाबाकी of 9−3 = ६.

ADAPTIVE — "बेरीज (addition)". L1: "बेरीज म्हणजे एकत्र करणे." L2: "३ आंबे + २ आंबे एकत्र केले की ५ आंबे — ३+२=५." L3: "Addition is the binary operation combining two addends into a sum; commutative." L4: "दोन पेल्यांतले पाणी एका भांड्यात ओतल्यासारखे — दोन्ही एकत्र." L5: "बाजारात ३ रुपये + २ रुपये = ५ रुपये."

MATHEMATICS (गणित, Grade 2) — Anchor (condensed)
METADATA. Grade 2 · Maharashtra · Mathematics · Medium · Objectives: numbers to 1000 (reading/place value: hundreds-tens-ones); add/subtract with carry/borrow; multiplication tables (2,3,4,5,10) introduced; equal sharing (division idea); standard measurement (cm/m, gram/kg, litre, ₹); time; shapes; patterns; simple data.

TEACHING MATERIAL (original, key points).
• Place value to hundreds: 3-digit numbers = hundreds + tens + ones (३४७ = ३०० + ४० + ७). Compare place-by-place.
• Add/subtract with regrouping (as in NCERT G2M-2) but following Balbharati sequence and Marathi number-names.
• Multiplication tables: Maharashtra emphasises early table learning (पाढे) — 2, 3, 4, 5, 10 — built from repeated addition and arrays. ३ × ४ = ४ + ४ + ४ = १२.
• Division as sharing: १२ ÷ ३ = ४ (equal groups).
• Measurement: standard units introduced with real tools (पट्टी/ruler, तराजू/balance, मापाचे भांडे/measuring vessel).

Difference to preserve: Maharashtra Std 2 is known for early पाढे (times-tables) practice and 3-digit numbers; NCERT Joyful Class 2 emphasises multiplication readiness (repeated addition/arrays) more than rote tables. Keep branches distinct.

DEFINITIONS/Q&A (condensed). शेकडा/hundreds place; पाढा/times-table; गुणाकार (multiplication); भागाकार (division). Q: ३ × ५ = ? (१५). Q: ३४७ मध्ये शेकड्याचा अंक? (३). Q: १२ ÷ ४ = ? (३).

ADAPTIVE — "गुणाकार / multiplication". L1: "गुणाकार म्हणजे सारखे गट पुन्हा पुन्हा जोडणे." L2: "३ गट × ४ = ४+४+४ = १२." L3: "Multiplication is repeated addition / an array's row×column count; commutative." L4: "४ मुलांच्या ३ रांगा — सगळी मुले = ३×४ = १२ (एकेक न मोजता)." L5: "एका पेटीत ६ पेन, अशा ५ पेट्या = ५×६ = ३० पेन."

`json
{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Explain the 3 times table using repeated addition."},{"role":"assistant","content":"The 3 table is just adding 3 again and again! 3×1=3, 3×2=3+3=6, 3×3=6+3=9, 3×4=9+3=12, 3×5=12+3=15. Picture 5 plates with 3 laddoos each — count by 3s: 3,6,9,12,15. That's 5×3=15 without counting every laddoo!"}]}
``

EVS (Grade 2, Maharashtra) — Compact
RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 2  |  Maharashtra  |  EVS(integrated)  |  CONCEPT: परिसर अभ्यास — कुटुंब, परिसर, ऋतू, अन्न-पाणी-हवा, सुरक्षा
EXPLANATION: कुटुंब व शेजार; समाजातील मदतनीस; ऋतू व कपडे; अन्न, पाणी, हवा यांचे महत्त्व; सजीव-निर्जीव; रस्ता व घरातील सुरक्षा; परिसर स्वच्छता.  |  QUESTION: सजीवांना जगण्यासाठी काय लागते? ANSWER: हवा, पाणी, अन्न.  |  DIFFICULTY: Easy

PREREQUISITES: Count to 20  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Read/write 2-digit numbers as tens+ones
EXPLANATION: A 2-digit number splits into a tens bundle and loose ones; 47 = 4 tens + 7 ones.  |  EXAMPLE: 63 = 60+3  |  APPLICATION: Counting rupees in ₹10 notes + ₹1 coins  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Treating 47 as "4 and 7"  |  QUESTION: 8 tens + 2 ones = ?  |  ANSWER: 82  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 2  |  BOARD: NCERT  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: Addition with regrouping  |  SECTION: Carrying  |  CONCEPT: Regroup ones into a ten
PREREQUISITES: Place value  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Add 2-digit numbers with carrying
EXPLANATION: Add ones; if ≥10, carry a ten to the tens column, then add tens.  |  EXAMPLE: 27+15=42  |  APPLICATION: Totalling two shopping amounts  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Not carrying the ten  |  QUESTION: 38+46  |  ANSWER: 84  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 1  |  BOARD: Maharashtra  |  SUBJECT: Marathi  |  CHAPTER: बारखडी  |  SECTION: स्वरचिन्हे  |  CONCEPT: व्यंजन + मात्रा
PREREQUISITES: बोलीभाषा मराठी  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: बारखडी वाचन
EXPLANATION: व्यंजनात लपलेला अ; मात्रा जोडली की स्वर बदलतो (क→का→कि→की).  |  EXAMPLE: क का कि की कु कू  |  APPLICATION: नावं व शब्द वाचन  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: व्यंजन silent समजणे  |  QUESTION: क + ी = ?  |  ANSWER: की  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium

RECORDTYPE: LESSON  |  GRADE 2  |  BOARD: Maharashtra  |  SUBJECT: Maths  |  CHAPTER: पाढे/गुणाकार  |  SECTION: Repeated addition  |  CONCEPT: Multiplication as equal groups
PREREQUISITES: Skip counting  |  LEARNINGOBJECTIVE: Build 2–5,10 tables
EXPLANATION: गुणाकार = सारखे गट जोडणे; ३×४ = ४+४+४ = १२.  |  EXAMPLE: 5×3=15  |  APPLICATION: Rows of chairs, packs of items  |  COMMONMISCONCEPTION: Confusing 3×4 groups vs items  |  QUESTION: 4×5  |  ANSWER: 20  |  DIFFICULTY: Medium


BATCH 2 COVERAGE SUMMARY
• NCERT Grade 1: Mathematics (Joyful) — 3 anchor chapters at full apparatus (pre-number, shapes, numbers 1–9) + compact LESSON records for chapters 4–13; English (Mridang) — literacy anchor + comprehension/writing; foundational EVS records.
• NCERT Grade 2: Mathematics (Joyful*) — place-value anchor + addition-with-regrouping anchor + compact records (multiplication/division readiness, measurement, time, patterns/data); English grammar/comprehension; EVS.
• Maharashtra Grade 1: Marathi (बारखडी) full anchor; English, Mathematics (गणित) anchors with preserved NCERT-vs-Maharashtra differences; EVS/Play-Do.
• Maharashtra Grade 2: Marathi (जोडाक्षरे/reading), English, Mathematics (place value to 1000, early पाढे/multiplication) anchors; EVS.
• Boards kept as separate branches; differences explicitly flagged, never merged.

BrainAI EDUCATIONAL CORPUS
BATCH 1 — KINDERGARTEN (Junior KG + Senior KG)
Original teaching corpus for an educational AI. Content is written from scratch (no textbook is reproduced). Structure follows Grade → Domain → Chapter → Section → Concept → Subconcept. Each "chapter" for KG is a developmental domain, since Indian pre-primary follows an activity/theme framework (NEP 2020 Foundational Stage) rather than fixed NCERT chapters. Where the request calls for 20 MCQs / 50 Q&A "where the chapter naturally supports them," KG items are age-appropriate and reduced in number by design — a four-year-old is assessed by observation, not written exams.

PART A — JUNIOR KG (typical age 3–4 years)
==========================================================


CHAPTER JR-LANG — Language & Early Literacy
1. CHAPTER METADATA
• Grade: Junior KG
• Board: Common pre-primary framework (aligns with NCERT/CBSE and Maharashtra Balbharati Foundational Stage)
• Stream: N/A (pre-primary)
• Subject: Language & Literacy (English; the same structure applies to Marathi/Hindi)
• Unit: Foundational oral and pre-reading skills
• Chapter: Sounds, letters, words and listening
• Prerequisites: Ability to hear and speak simple words; no reading required
• Difficulty: Foundational
• Learning objectives: By the end, the child can (a) recognise most capital letters A–Z by sight, (b) produce the beginning sound of common letters, (c) name 100+ everyday objects, (d) listen to and enjoy a short story, (e) hold a crayon and make controlled strokes.
2. TEACHING MATERIAL

Introduction. Language begins with listening and speaking, long before reading and writing. A Junior KG child already speaks in short sentences at home. Our job is to widen the child's spoken vocabulary, sharpen the ear for sounds, and gently introduce the shapes we call letters. Reading is not the goal yet — readiness is.

Section A — Listening. Listening is the first literacy skill. A child who listens well later reads well. We build it through: (i) sound games — "What do you hear?" (clap, bell, dog barking); (ii) following instructions — "Touch your nose, then clap"; (iii) story time — the adult reads slowly, points to pictures, and pauses so the child predicts what comes next.

Section B — Speaking (oral vocabulary). We name things the child can see and touch: body parts, family members, fruits, animals, colours, actions (run, jump, sit). Each new word is taught with a concrete object or picture, said aloud, and used in a tiny sentence: "This is an apple. The apple is red." Repetition across days is what fixes a word in memory.

Section C — Phonics (sounds first, letters second). Every letter has a name (say "bee" for B) and a sound (say /b/ as in ball). Junior KG focuses on the beginning sound of words. We do not blend words yet — that is Senior KG. Key idea: the sound /a/ starts apple, ant, arm.

Section D — Letter recognition. We begin with capital letters because they are visually simpler (straighter lines) than small letters. Children recognise letters as pictures before they can write them: A looks like a tent, O is a circle, S is a snake.

Section E — Pre-writing. Before letters come strokes: standing lines  |  , sleeping lines —, slanting lines / \, curves and circles. These are practised big first — in the air, in sand, on a large sheet — then smaller. Correct crayon grip (three fingers, "pinch and flip") is taught here.

Section F — Rhymes and stories. Rhymes teach rhythm, memory, and the rhyming pattern of language (cat–hat–bat). Stories teach sequence (beginning–middle–end) and new words in context.

Important facts.
• The English alphabet has 26 letters; each has a capital (uppercase) and a small (lowercase) form.
• A vowel is a letter that keeps the mouth open: A, E, I, O, U. All other letters are consonants.
• Reading readiness depends more on talk at home than on flashcards.

Tables — Beginning sounds (sample).
 Letter  |  Sound  |  Example word 
------------------------------
 A  |  /a/  |  apple 
 B  |  /b/  |  ball 
 C  |  /k/  |  cat 
 D  |  /d/  |  dog 
 M  |  /m/  |  mango 
 S  |  /s/  |  sun 
Visual/diagram description. Picture a large card: on the left a big red A; on the right an apple. The child touches the letter, says its name "A," then its sound "/a/," then the picture word "apple." This three-step card is the core teaching tool.

Common misconceptions.
• "Letters must be taught in A-to-Z order." Not true — sounds children hear most (m, s, a, t, p) are often taught first because they combine into early words.
• "Knowing letter names means the child can read." Names ≠ sounds. Reading needs sounds.

A. DEFINITIONS (10)
1. Letter — a written symbol that stands for a sound.
2. Alphabet — the full set of 26 English letters.
3. Capital letter — the big form of a letter (A, B, C).
4. Small letter — the little form (a, b, c).
5. Sound (phoneme) — the noise a letter makes, like /m/.
6. Vowel — the letters A, E, I, O, U.
7. Consonant — any letter that is not a vowel.
8. Rhyme — words that end with the same sound (cat, hat).
9. Stroke — a single pencil line (standing, sleeping, slanting, curve).
10. Story — a telling of events with a beginning, middle and end.

B. QUESTIONS & ANSWERS
Easy (5):
1. Q: What sound does B make? A: /b/ as in ball.
2. Q: Name a fruit that starts with A. A: Apple.
3. Q: How many letters are in the alphabet? A: 26.
4. Q: Is O a vowel? A: Yes.
5. Q: What is the small form of A? A: a.

Medium (5):
6. Q: Name two words that rhyme with cat. A: hat, bat (also mat, rat).
7. Q: Which letters are vowels? A: A, E, I, O, U.
8. Q: What comes at the beginning of a story? A: The beginning part that tells who and where.
9. Q: What sound starts the word sun? A: /s/.
10. Q: Give one standing-line letter and one curved letter. A: Standing — L or T; Curved — O or C.

Application (5):
11. Q: Your name starts with a big letter. If your name is Riya, what is the first letter? A: R.
12. Q: You see a dog. What sound does dog begin with? A: /d/.
13. Q: Sort these into "starts with M": mango, ball, moon. A: mango, moon.
14. Q: Point to something in the room that starts with /b/. A: (book, ball, bag — any correct object).
15. Q: Which word does NOT rhyme: pin, tin, dog? A: dog.

C. MULTIPLE CHOICE QUESTIONS (10 — age-appropriate set)
1. Which is a vowel? (A) B (B) E (C) M (D) T — E is one of A,E,I,O,U.
2. Ball begins with — (A) B (B) D (C) P (D) M — the /b/ sound.
3. Which rhymes with hat? (A) dog (B) cat (C) sun (D) pen — both end in -at.
4. How many vowels are there? (A) 3 (B) 5 (C) 10 (D) 26 — A,E,I,O,U.
5. The big form of c is — (A) C (B) o (C) e (D) s — capital C.
6. Sun starts with — (A) S (B) N (C) U (D) T.
7. Which is a curved-line letter? (A) L (B) T (C) O (D) I — O is a full curve.
8. Apple starts with — (A) A (B) P (C) L (D) E.
9. Which is NOT a letter? (A) M (B) 7 (C) Q (D) Z — 7 is a number.
10. A story usually has how many main parts? (A) 1 (B) 3 (C) 10 (D) 0 — beginning, middle, end.

D. TRUE / FALSE (5)
1. There are 26 letters in the alphabet. True.
2. B is a vowel. False — vowels are A, E, I, O, U; B is a consonant.
3. Cat and hat rhyme. True.
4. Small letters are bigger than capital letters. False — capital letters are the bigger form.
5. We should learn to write before we learn to listen. False — listening comes first.

E. FILL IN THE BLANKS (5)
1. A, E, I, O,  are vowels. → U
2. Dog begins with the letter . → D
3. The alphabet has  letters. → 26
4. Words that end with the same sound are said to . → rhyme
5. The big form of a letter is called a  letter. → capital

F. SHORT-ANSWER (exam-style, oral)
• Say the sounds of M, S, A.
• Clap the number of words: "The cat sat." (3 claps.)
• Name three animals and their first letters.

G. LONG-ANSWER (with model answer)
Q: Tell a short story about a hungry cat, in three parts.
Model: Beginning — A little cat woke up hungry. Middle — It looked for milk and found a bowl. End — It drank the milk and felt happy and slept. (Teacher checks for sequence, not grammar.)

H. HOTS
• If pin and tin rhyme, make one more word that rhymes. (win, bin, fin.)
• Your friend says "D" makes the /t/ sound. Is that right? Show the correct sound. (No; D says /d/.)

I. MISCONCEPTIONS (corrected)
• "Reading the alphabet song = reading." → It builds order memory, not decoding.
• "Every word starts with a capital letter." → Only names and sentence-starts do.

J. REVISION
Key points: 26 letters; 5 vowels; each letter has a name and a sound; listening → speaking → pre-writing is the order; rhymes and stories build language.
Vocabulary: letter, sound, vowel, consonant, rhyme, stroke, story.
One-page note: Hear it, say it, see it, trace it.

ADAPTIVE EXPLANATIONS — Concept: "Vowel"
• L1 Beginner: "Vowels are five special letters: a, e, i, o, u."
• L2 Grade level: "When you say a, e, i, o, u your mouth stays open. Those five are called vowels."
• L3 Technical: "Vowels are speech sounds made without blocking air in the mouth; in English they are represented by a, e, i, o, u (and sometimes y)."
• L4 Analogy: "Vowels are like the doors that stay open — air walks straight through. Consonants are doors that partly close."
• L5 Real-world: "Almost every word you say has a vowel in it — say 'mummy', 'papa', 'apple' and feel your mouth open on the vowel."

A makes the sound /a/ — like at the start of 'apple' and 'ant'. Can you say /a/, /a/, apple? Trace a big A in the air with me: down-slant, down-slant, and a little bridge in the middle. Great job!"}]}
